How to Scrape Instagram Comments With an API (curl + Python to CSV)

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Getting all the comments from a public Instagram post sounds like an afternoon of work. It usually is not. I build SocialHolmes, a social data API, so I deal with this every day. Here is why it is awkward, which options you really have, and a small script that saves any public post's comments to CSV.

1. The official Instagram Graph API. If you want comments on your own business or creator account, use it. It is the right tool: free, supported, stable. The catch is that it only returns comments on accounts connected to your app. It cannot fetch comments on someone else's public post, which is what most research, competitor and giveaway use cases need.

2. DIY scraping (Playwright/Selenium, or replaying the GraphQL calls). You get full control and no per-row fee, but you own the whole maintenance burden: login sessions, proxies, selector fixes, retries. Fine for a one-off; painful as a pipeline.

3. A paid scraping API. Someone else maintains the scraper and you call an endpoint. You trade money for time. I use SocialHolmes for the rest of this post (disclosure: I build it), but the pattern of start a run, poll, read pages is common to most of these services.

Grab the post's shortcode: the part after /p/ in the URL. In instagram.com/p/C1a2B3cDEfG/ it is C1a2B3cDEfG.

Sign up for an API key (1,000 free results, no card), then export it:

You get back 202 Accepted and a run object with an id and a status. limit is the most comments you want. It is also the most you can be billed.

Statuses are queued, running, succeeded, empty, failed and cancelled. Comments come back as JSON, up to 100 per page; use offset to page.

If you do not want to write code at all, the dashboard has a form (Instagram, Comments, paste the shortcode) and a Download CSV button.

If you use Claude or another MCP client, SocialHolmes exposes an MCP server, so you can skip the script and ask "get the first 200 comments on instagram.com/p/C1a2B3cDEfG". It runs the same endpoint and costs the same.

Pricing is $0.50 per 1,000 results on every endpoint, and you pay only for rows returned. A run that returns nothing costs nothing. Some numbers:

New accounts get 1,000 free results, which is enough to try this on a handful of posts.

Comments are public, but a stored record of who said what is personal data under GDPR and similar laws. Stick to public data, respect rate limits, think about whether you really need usernames (the text and timestamp are often enough), and check the law and Instagram's terms for your jurisdiction and use case. This is not legal advice.

If you have hit an Instagram scraping edge case I did not cover, tell me in the comments and I will try to reproduce it.

Why Your Personal Knowledge Base Should Be Local-First and AI-Friendly

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

We've become incredibly good at collecting information.

Articles, bookmarks, screenshots, PDFs, research papers, code snippets, ideas, and random thoughts. There's always something worth saving.

But there's a problem: most of this information ends up scattered across different applications.

Your notes are in one app, bookmarks in another, documents in cloud storage, and screenshots somewhere in your file system.

Over time, managing information becomes almost as difficult as finding it.

I think the next generation of personal knowledge management tools should approach this problem differently.

They should be local-first, built around portable data formats, and accessible to AI agents.

Most productivity applications provide a convenient experience by managing your information behind the scenes.

You create notes, organize folders, add tags, and everything works.

Depending on the application, exporting your information might mean downloading archives, converting proprietary formats, or losing relationships between documents.

Your information technically belongs to you, but accessing it outside the application can be difficult.

This creates unnecessary dependency on a single software provider.

A personal knowledge base should be something you can maintain independently of the application used to access it.

Local-first software prioritizes keeping data on your own device while allowing applications to provide a convenient interface for working with it.

Instead of treating the cloud as the only place where your information exists, your local device becomes an important part of the architecture.

Local-first doesn't automatically mean every application is private or secure. Implementation matters.

But the underlying philosophy is valuable: your data should not become inaccessible simply because an application changes its business model or disappears.

Markdown is one of the simplest ways to make information portable.

A Markdown file is essentially a plain-text document with lightweight formatting conventions.

You can open this file in a text editor, code editor, Markdown application, or process it programmatically.

Portability: Your notes aren't locked into a proprietary format.

Longevity: Plain text is easy to preserve and migrate.

Interoperability: Many applications and development tools support Markdown.

Automation: Scripts and AI agents can read and modify Markdown files.

This makes Markdown particularly interesting for people who want more control over their personal knowledge base.

AI agents are changing how we interact with software.

Previously, we manually searched through documents, copied information, and organized everything ourselves.

Now, agents can perform many of these tasks.

But there's a fundamental requirement: agents need access to your information.

Consider a workspace where your notes, research, and resources are stored in accessible files.

You don't necessarily need an AI chatbot built into every productivity application.

Sometimes, making your data accessible through a filesystem or CLI is enough to let external AI tools work with it.

This is an interesting design direction: instead of building another isolated AI experience, make existing software interoperable with the agents people already use.

A lot of knowledge management applications are built around documents, folders, and hierarchical organization.

That works well when you're writing structured notes.

But research and brainstorming aren't always linear activities.

When exploring an unfamiliar topic, you might have:

An infinite canvas offers another way to work with this information.

Instead of organizing everything into a hierarchy, you can arrange resources spatially.

Related information can live together. Different concepts can form clusters. Your workspace can evolve as your understanding develops.

This is especially useful for research, brainstorming, visual planning, and connecting ideas.

I think a good knowledge workspace should combine a few fundamental principles:

These principles don't require an extremely complicated application.

In fact, the best implementation might be surprisingly simple: a flexible workspace sitting on top of accessible files.

This is the approach I've been exploring with Toxvas, a local-first infinite canvas for macOS.

Toxvas combines visual organization with file-based storage.

Its cards are Markdown files, and you can place different kinds of content on your canvas, including links with previews, videos, images, PDFs, and other files.

You can also record audio directly on the canvas.

One feature I'm particularly interested in is its agent CLI.

It allows AI agents to work with the Toxvas workspace through its files, making the workspace more accessible to external tools.

The idea is simple: your knowledge shouldn't be trapped inside an application. It should be something you can interact with using different tools and workflows.

If you're interested in this approach to visual knowledge management, you can explore Toxvas here:

Toxvas — Local-First Infinite Canvas for Mac

I don't think the future of knowledge management is about building increasingly complicated note-taking applications.

It's about reducing friction between collecting information, understanding it, connecting ideas, and actually using what we've learned.

Local-first architecture, portable formats like Markdown, visual workspaces, and AI interoperability are interesting pieces of that direction.

Your knowledge base should be an asset you own, not another collection of data trapped inside an application.

Building Orquest at SharkByte: Turning Career Planning Into an AI-Powered Game 🦈🎮

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This weekend, my team and I participated in SharkByte Hackathon, where we challenged ourselves to build something that could actually help students.

One problem we kept thinking about was career planning. As students, we're constantly making decisions about classes, degrees, and our future, but sometimes we don't even know what career we want to pursue.

What if figuring out your future felt more like playing a game?

That's how Orquest — A Game for Your Future! was born.

Orquest is an AI-powered career discovery game that helps students explore their interests, discover possible careers, and build personalized educational pathways.

Instead of starting with a degree, we wanted students to discover the jobs that interest them first.

The idea is to make career planning something students can actively explore instead of just reading a list of recommendations.

We built Orquest using React, JavaScript, Vite, Python, and FastAPI.

We integrated Google Gemini API for career guidance and used GitHub to manage our team's work.

We also experimented with Swarms API and Prune API, successfully testing a three-agent Swarms workflow and collecting a verified signed Prune report.

For deployment, we used Docker and Render.

During development, we worked with OpenAI Codex, GitHub Copilot, and Base44 to help us build, debug, and improve different parts of the application.

One of the hardest parts wasn't writing the code. It was making everything work together.

At one point, our frontend couldn't communicate with our backend because of a CORS issue.

Then we spent a lot of time trying to get Gemini working. Our API requests kept failing until we discovered a problem with the JSON schema we were sending.

We also had to manage different GitHub branches, integrate our teammates' work, and deal with AI development tool limits. When one of our teammates ran out of Codex credits, he switched to Base44 to continue improving the UI.

And of course, we were doing all of this while trying to stay within a $0 out-of-pocket budget.

Eventually, we got Orquest deployed publicly, and seeing Gemini generate a real career response through our website was one of the best moments of the hackathon.

This hackathon taught me that software development is much more than knowing how to program.

You need to understand how different systems connect, how to investigate errors, how to communicate with your team, and how to make decisions when time is running out.

I also learned a lot about REST APIs, AI integrations, frontend/backend communication, testing, and deployment.

One of my biggest takeaways was that getting something to work is exciting, but understanding why it works is even more valuable.

Orquest is still growing. We want to improve the user experience, expand our verified educational pathways, and eventually let students save their career progress permanently.

This weekend showed me how much a team can accomplish when everyone brings different skills and ideas together.

I'm grateful for the experience and everything we learned along the way.

GitHub: https://github.com/Oliver102274/Orquest

L’avviso ai naviganti proprio per Genova: cosa Amorim si attende dal Milan

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

L’avviso ai naviganti era già stato inviato da Ruben Amorim poco dopo la pessima partita contro il Ferencvaros: “Dobbiamo concentrarci sui piccoli dettagli, senza pensare alla Champions League o a cose simili. Dobbiamo solo focalizzarci sul miglioramento degli aspetti più specifici del calcio: la mentalità, il cuore, la passione. Dobbiamo migliorare in questo". In questi due giorni a Milanello, il tecnico portoghese ha parlato alla squadra nel privato dello spogliatoio, ricordando a tutti che, senza spirito, non si va da nessuna parte anche se si dovesse avere una rosa fatta di soli campioni. E, siccome il Milan non fa parte di questa categoria, c’è bisogno sempre di aggiungere impegno e passione, soprattutto nelle partite contro le squadre di più basso livello.

Per questo motivo, Ruben Amorim oggi si aspetta delle risposte. A Genova, contro Daniele De Rossi, non sarà facile, perché i rossoblu piazzano sempre sul campo di Marassi tanta intensità e tanto agonismo. Il Milan, oltre alle qualità tecniche superiori, dovrà rimettere in campo tutto ciò che a livello di spirito è mancato in Europa League. Tornano i titolarissimi dopo il primo tempo di riposo di giovedì: dentro dal primo minuto Gila in difesa assieme al confermato De Winter, Modric con Musah e Rabiot a centrocampo, Moreira e Pulisic in attacco alle spalle di Gonçalo Ramos, inamovibile davanti. Pavlovic non sarà convocato e quindi le scelte diventano quasi obbligate: Diawara ancora al centro della difesa e a sinistra uno fra Estupinan e Bartesaghi. In porta torna Maignan, con la fascia di capitano al braccio, dopo aver saltato per squalifica la partita contro il Ferencvaros. Assenti Gabbia, Saelemaekers, Chukwueze, P. Terracciano, Tomori, Loftus-Cheek e appunto Pavlovic.

L'Inter è davvero così superiore al Como? Il confronto delle rose, in tutti i reparti

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Il piatto forte dell'11^ giornata di Serie A arriva domenica alle 18, con la sfida di altissima classifica tra Inter e Como. Il confronto di San Siro mette contro i campioni d'Italia in carica e la realtà che più di ogni altra nelle ultime stagioni ha saputo scalare gradini con costanza. Per questo una domanda ci è venuta spontanea, specie dopo la notte da record vissuta a Lens in Champions League dai lariani: siamo sicuri che la differenza di valori tra le due rose esista e sia così netta? Proviamo a dare uno sguardo alla composizione delle due squadre.

Il primo dubbio viene già in porta. Dove l'Inter non è sembrata ancora superare l'era Sommer, mentre in riva al lago c'è la sensazione di avere invece un tandem di primi portieri interscambiabili e sempre pronti all'uso. Osservando i valori di Transfermarkt, il Como ha maggiori risorse rispetto all'Inter tra i pali. E ci sentiamo di concordare anche in termini di valore tecnico assoluto.

Inter 13,3 milioni (10 J. Martinez, 3 Provedel, 0,3 Di Gennaro) Como 23,2 milioni (15 Sanchez 8 Butez 0,2 Vigorito)

In difesa ci si imbatte invece nel primo duello nettamente a favore dei nerazzurri. Se c'è un reparto in cui in effetti si misura grande differenza, quello è la difesa. Gli otto rappresentanti dell'Inter hanno un valore complessivo che è quasi il doppio, secondo il portale specializzato, rispetto ai dieci della squadra di Fabregas. Leggendo i nomi, anche qui sembra esserci ben poco da eccepire.

Inter 264 milioni (65 Bastoni, 50 Bisseck, 50 Dimarco, 35 Spence, 23 C. Augusto, 17 Akanji, 12 Stones, 12 Pavard) Como 140 milioni (45 Ramon 35 Chalobah 17 Couto 14 Kaiki 12 Valle 5 Dossena 3,5 Kempf 3,5 Smolcic 3 Kambwala 2 Goldaniga)

Proseguendo verso il centrocampo, un'altra sorpresa: i dati Transfermarkt vedono i nove del Como valere più degli altrettanti colleghi di reparto di casa Inter. Paz da solo vale 80 milioni, ma ci sono anche Baturina, Perrone e Da Cunha a rendere concreto il sorpasso nei confronti di Barella, Calhanoglu (il cui valore tende verso il basso per via della situazione contrattuale) e compagni.

Inter 241 milioni (50 Barella 45 Sucic 35 Jones 32 Stankovic 30 Diouf 20 L. Henrique 16 Calhanoglu 10 Zielinski 3 Mkhitaryan) Como 263 milioni (80 Paz 55 Baturina 35 Perrone 30 Da Cunha 22 Ricci 13 Caqueret 10 Lahdo 10 Liberali 8 Milla)

Chiudiamo il giro con l'attacco, dove i valori dati dagli esperti del settore vedono una nuova predominanza nerazzurra. Poco da dire sulla supremazia di Lautaro Martinez sui componenti di ciascuna delle due realtà, qualcuno in più sul reale valore di Douvikas, al momento stimato in un ingeneroso ammontare di 25 milioni di euro. La panoramica sulle rose di Inter (738,3 milioni di euro totali) e Como (568,2), comunque, ci suggerisce che il distacco tra esse sia tutt'altro che abissale.

Inter 220 milioni (85 Lautaro 50 Thuram 50 Pio Esposito 35 Bonny) Como 142 milioni (35 Diao 32 Kean 30 J. Rodriguez 25 Douvikas 20 Addai)

Red Raiders win a wild one over WVU | Texas Tech football report card

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Thomas Castellanos threw for career highs of 362 yards and 5 touchdowns, the last a go-ahead 5-yard throw to Kenny Johnson with 2:44 left as the Texas Tech football team rallied to beat West Virginia 53-48 on Saturday, Nov. 7, at Galaxy Stadium.

Johnson's second TD catch of the game capped a 9-play, 69-yard drive kept alive by a leaping fourth-down grab by Micah Hudson four plays earlier. Castellanos threw the ball into traffic over the middle — for Terrance Carter Jr., it appeared — and the 26-yard catch moved the ball to the WVU 26-yard line.

After Johnson's TD put Tech ahead 51-48, West Virginia turned the ball over on downs with 1:55 to go, and then quarterback Michael Hawkins Jr. was tackled for a safety with 1:24 left. Edge defender Trey White made the play on both.

More: WR Coy Eakin leaves Texas Tech football game vs. West Virginia

More: Texas Tech football RB Cameron Dickey aggravates ankle injury

Tech (8-1, 5-1 in the Big 12) set a school record with its 14th home victory in a row. The Red Raiders gained sole possession of second place in the Big 12, one game behind Utah (9-0, 6-0).

J'Koby Williams rushed for 160 yards and a touchdown on 19 carries. Castellanos, making his second start in a row over Will Hammond, finished with 102 yards on 23 attempts.

Castellanos' other scoring passes went for 30 and 29 yards to Carter, for 1 yard to Jett Carpenter and for 38 yards to Johnson.

West Virginia (5-4, 2-4) piled up 582 yards total offense, but the Mountaineers failed in Rich Rodriguez's first chance to get his 200th career coaching victory.

Hawkins completed 11 of 16 passes for 303 yards. He ran 19 times for 88 yards and 3 touchdowns, giving him 19 for the season.

The two teams combined for 1,220 yards of total offense. At halftime, they'd combined for 892 yards and Castellanos had 4 TD passes, tying the career high he set while playing for Boston College against Duquesne in a September 2024 game.

Red Raiders put up a season high in total offense, but needed the game-winning drive to atone for a four-series stretch after halftime that all ended in punts. Williams topped 100 yards rushing for the third time in four games.

West Virginia's 582 yards were a multi-year high against a Shiel Wood defense. Busts and big plays were an issue as Tech allowed seven plays, including five passes, longer than 30 yards.

Red Raiders yielded a 75-yard punt return that set up a go-ahead touchdown in the third quarter. Kenny Johnson punt returns being erased by penalty is a recurring problem. Punter Will Karoll averaged 42.2 yards, but hasn't seemed the same in the second half of the season.

The Tech defense didn't look well-coached in for about three quarters, and the offense conked out for a while. The staff rallied both units when the game was on the line.

The Red Raiders had to have this one to keep their Big 12 championship and College Football Playoff hopes on solid footing. They'll be under just as much pressure next week, though, facing Oklahoma State's prolific passing offense on the road.

This article originally appeared on Lubbock Avalanche-Journal: Red Raiders win a wild one over WVU | Texas Tech football report card

InfoQ Homepage News Cloudflare Scales D1 Control Plane to Millions of Databases

Nov 08, 2026 2 min read

In a recent article, Cloudflare shared the redesign of the control plane of its D1 serverless SQL database to support millions of databases, avoiding centralized bottlenecks, implementing retries and idempotency, and using eventual consistency.

The D1 control plane architecture is built around Postgres for transactional metadata, ClickHouse for analytics, and Workers KV for globally replicated routing data. It keeps query processing at the edge, separate from centralized management operations, using distributed metadata caching, connection pooling, asynchronous workflows, and periodic health checks to improve scalability and recover from failures. Announced in 2022, Cloudflare D1 is a serverless SQL database built on SQLite and designed to run close to applications on Cloudflare’s global network. Over the past year, the number of daily active databases has grown 18-fold.

Cloudflare uses 30-second heartbeats, with per-database jitter, to reconcile each D1 Durable Object with Postgres, the source of truth for database existence and account limits. This mechanism makes failed creation or deletion operations self-correcting: orphaned objects are marked as deleted, and Workers KV routing metadata is repaired if best-effort updates fail. David Laban, full stack software engineer at Cloudflare, and Lambros Petrou, principal engineer at Cloudflare, write:

Heartbeats can tolerate slightly out-of-date data, meaning they can be served by Hyperdrive’s multiple caching layers or by a Postgres read replica. This heartbeat flow enables the Durable Object to self-correct to reflect the source of truth (Postgres) regarding the creation and deletion of databases.

Cloudflare keeps D1 queries off its centralized Postgres database by routing requests from an Edge API Worker through an Eyeball Worker directly to the relevant D1 Durable Object.

The Edge API Worker handles authentication, authorization, rate limiting, and request validation, while the Eyeball Worker trusts the database-specific authorization encoded in D1 Bindings, avoiding redundant checks in the query path. Laban and Petrou add:

You might also wonder why we use Workers KV when we already have Hyperdrive Caching. Workers KV is a durable datastore, not just a cache: if an item falls out of KV's edge cache, the read is served from KV's own storage rather than Postgres, keeping Postgres out of the data plane. In a catastrophic cache purge scenario, a million databases trying to fetch their routing information would lead to metastable failures.

Cloudflare enforces D1 storage limits using asynchronous usage updates, which are queued and batched to protect Postgres from write spikes. It maintains pre-aggregated account totals through incremental storage deltas, avoiding expensive scans and write contention for accounts with millions of databases. Durable Objects check these totals through Hyperdrive’s short-lived cache and block writes that would exceed the account limit. In a Reddit thread, user isaackogan comments:

It's an impressive architecture to be sure, but most of these gains are that it's an in-process database running on edge storage near you. The rest is orchestration around that. Cloudflare can leverage their massive global datacenter presence to do something that would be traditionally impossible for small shops, aka economies of scale at work.

In a previous popular thread, several Reddit users argued that D1 is unreliable, raising concerns about its availability and stability in production workloads.

Cloudflare D1 offers a permanent free tier that includes 5 GB of total storage, 5 million rows read per day, and 100,000 rows written per day. Beyond the free tier, storage costs 0.75 USD per GB-month, reads cost $0.001 per million rows, and writes cost 1 USD per million rows. D1 has no compute or data-transfer charges.

HBO's 36-Episode Sci-Fi Series Is A Masterclass In How Mystery Boxes Can Go Wrong

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Few storytelling structures changed the TV landscape quite like the mystery box. The format drops viewers into a world filled with the unexplained, then spends several seasons delivering a drip-feed of answers. When done right, they’re incredibly satisfying. A strange detail in the premiere might seem like nothing until an episode two seasons later gives it a new meaning. Mystery boxes exploded in popularity in the 2000s thanks to addictive hits like Lost, though they’re far from a guaranteed recipe for success.

The intrigue that sets up a mystery box show in its opening episodes is easy to construct. Giving viewers a reason to want to keep revisiting it, however, is a different story. An opening episode or even season only needs a handful of strange clues to get the ball rolling, but the answers have to be worth the wait when they finally arrive. What’s more, the characters need lives and problems that make the central mystery one that audiences want to solve. Few shows proved how difficult a balancing act this is quite like HBO’s Westworld.

Westworld aired for four seasons from 2016 to 2022 before being abruptly canceled, and it's sadly remembered for all the wrong reasons. The sci-fi series begins its 36-episode run in a Wild West theme park where wealthy guests can indulge their fantasies at the expense of lifelike androids called hosts. However, all was not as it seemed thanks to the hosts being considerably more aware of their situation than they should have been. At first, Westworld seemed like it had all it needed to become the next Lost. Sadly, by the time it ended, the HBO show’s contribution to pop culture was being a case study in how not to do a mystery box story.

Westworld’s mystery box certainly was full of mystery. The problem was that mystery was pretty much all it was full of. The answers the show delivered were often simply new questions in disguise, and after a while it felt like the HBO show was more interested in keeping audiences guessing than it was in telling an enjoyable story. As the puzzles grew more and more elaborate, even figuring out where a scene fit into the timeline could take enough effort to make it borderline unwatchable. Non-chronological storytelling wasn't Westworld's greatest sin when it came to misunderstanding what makes its format appealing, either.

As Westworld went on, many reveals that were clearly meant to be impactful completely fell flat because viewers are still trying to figure out exactly what kind of show they’re watching and what the story was actually about. The cinematography, intense performances, and dramatic music underpinning a conversation would make it clear that it’s integral to the plot, but audiences couldn’t appreciate it because the show hadn’t made it clear enough why the scene mattered. Mystery box shows should leave their viewers asking questions, but the question should never be “why should I care”. Sadly, for Westworld, this increasingly became the biggest mystery surrounding its narrative.

Westworld fell into this trap because its compulsive need to construct a grand narrative puzzle came at the expense of character development. This meant that viewers inevitably cared less and less about solving the show’s mysteries with each passing episode. Understanding why a character reacts the way they do to a new reveal is integral to a mystery box being engaging viewing. Westworld’s underdeveloped characters removed this essential grounding, meaning that even its most shocking twists had minimal emotional impact.

This overreliance on the mysteries in its mystery box is why Westworld lost momentum so quickly after its admittedly solid first season. A new reveal or mysterious tease of things to come should make audiences want to see what happens next. When these twists and turns happen to characters viewers simply don’t care about in a story that’s too difficult to follow, however, the appeal of the mystery box format dwindles and dies incredibly quickly.

The increasingly elaborate puzzles and lack of substantial character development are only part of why Westworld was a failure. The other part was how dramatically HBO’s breakout hit changed from the intrigue of its central premise. The idea of a group of autonomous AI that exist as theme park attractions gradually gaining sentience was a fascinating one. Sadly, Westworld moved away from this setting and the narrative arcs that made its story followable after its first season. By the time it circled back to its original premise it was too little too late, as the show had already lost much of its audience.

A pivotal moment when it comes to Westworld’s downfall was in season 3, when the show flipped from being a uniquely distinctive sci-fi Western to a borderline-generic cyberpunk thriller. The park’s familiar Western routines had made the cruelty the AI hosts suffer at the hands of guests and their awakening after believing that they genuinely lived in the 19th century incredibly unsettling. Once the show moved to a futuristic city, Westworld felt like yet another near-future sci-fi show about the dangers of technology. The change meant the series lost everything unique about its identity.

Ultimately, HBO canceled Westworld after season 4, with its central story unresolved, then pulled it from HBO Max. All four seasons are available to buy digitally, but they’re not available to stream on any major platform. This speaks volumes about Westworld’s legacy as a blueprint for what not to do when it comes to mystery box storytelling. Many initially-divisive sci-fi shows manage to find pop-culture footholds years after they concluded thanks to arriving on a streaming service. Westworld’s legacy is the total opposite. It’s a show that HBO would clearly rather audiences forgot about, but its spectacular fall from grace ensures it will always be an infamous part of TV history.

iPad mini 8 vs. Kindle Colorsoft: These are two of the best devices I’ve used for reading, but one might be better for you than the other — I’ll help you decide which one to get

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Two fantastic devices, but which is the best for reading?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I recently reviewed the iPad mini 8, and it has quickly become one of my favorite devices for reading. That’s saying a lot, considering how I’ve been an avid Kindle reader for over ten years. That said, the Kindle Colorsoft Signature Edition also offers a brilliant reading experience.

The iPad mini 8 has many great features for reading. It’s slimmer and lighter, making it easier to hold for long reading sessions. The new OLED panel helps text and color images pop, and the nano-texture display option gives the panel a more paper-like feel.

Even if the iPad mini 8 is more feature-rich, the Kindle Colorsoft is still a fantastic e-reader thanks to its light and sturdy build, large color display, and smooth integration with the Kindle ecosystem. And even the more expensive Signature Edition is still hundreds of dollars less than an iPad mini 8.

You can read my iPad mini 8 review and Kindle Colorsoft Signature Edition review to learn more — but suffice it to say, these are among the best for reading. But which one is right for you? I’ll break down everything you need to know to help you decide.

The iPad mini 8 is the most significant upgrade to Apple’s smallest tablet. The thinner, lighter design makes it more portable than ever, while the new 8.5-inch OLED panel delivers stunning image quality for videos and books. Powered by the A20 Pro chip, it also delivers fast performance and more-than-all-day battery life. Though pricier and missing features like Face ID and a 120Hz refresh rate, it still delivers a first-rate iPad experience.

The Amazon Kindle Colorsoft Signature Edition (2026) improves on its predecessor with a thinner, lighter design and more vibrant color display. It’s arguably the best color e-reader right now, especially for Kindle users, though its steep price may deter some. But for what it delivers as a color e-reader, it’s hard to top.

The iPad mini 8 starts at $699 for the 128GB model with a glossy screen. You can upgrade to a model with the glare-reducing nano-texture display, but that requires upgrading to 256GB, which adds $200 to the overall cost.

Conversely, the Amazon Kindle Signature Edition costs $319 with 32GB of storage and no ad support. The standard Kindle Colorsoft with 16GB of storage and without the aluminum back housing costs $289.

The price difference between the iPad mini 8 and Kindle Colorsoft Signature Edition is substantial. Between the two, the Kindle is the more affordable option, though both are relatively expensive compared to their previous models.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

The new iPad mini 8 and Kindle Colorsoft have been redesigned to be thinner and lighter than their respective predecessors. As a result, they're easy to read for extended periods.

Of the two, the iPad mini 8 is larger and heavier, measuring 7.69 x 5.22 x 0.21 inches and weighing 8.9 ounces. The Kindle Colorsoft Signature Edition is still pretty sizeable at 6.9 x 5.0 x 0.3 inches and 8.1 ounces, but it’s decidedly smaller in comparison.

The iPad mini 8 is one single piece of machined aluminum, whereas the only aluminum part of the Kindle Colorsoft is its back housing. Still, both feel sturdy and smooth in hand. Both devices are also waterproof, with the iPad mini 8 rated IP67 and the Colorsoft rated IPX8. Colorwise, Apple’s miniature tablet comes in Midnight and Starlight, while the Kindle Colorsoft comes in Fig and Graphite.

The iPad mini 8 has the more premium design overall, but both are elegant slabs in their own right.

Here’s where things diverge sharply. Since we’re discussing a digital ink display against an OLED panel, which is admittedly not a one-to-one comparison, I’ll instead focus on the actual reading experience each provides to make things fairer.

The iPad mini 8’s display is a big improvement over the past, thanks to its larger 8.5-inch display, thinner bezels, and higher resolution of 2316 x 1512 pixels at 326 pixels per inch (ppi). Its OLED panel also delivers excellent contrast and vivid colors. Novels, comics, and everything else look fantastic.

Apple sent us a review unit with a nano-texture display, which makes the screen more comparable to a Kindle for reading by reducing glare. Plus, the texture itself gives it a more paper-like feel. While it stings that you have to pay $200 more for the 256GB model to get the nano-texture option, it’s a great feature.

The Kindle Colorsoft has the same 7-inch 300ppi color display as the previous model, but Amazon has employed a new reverse-stack display construction to make the screen-to-bezel transition more seamless.

While black-and-white content looks brighter and bolder on a Kindle Paperwhite due to this reader’s color layer, colors are bolder than before. Whether it’s books or comics, everything looks great. However, color reading drops the resolution to 150ppi.

Though the iPad mini 8 might give you more eye strain since it can get significantly brighter, it still has the more vibrant display of the two. However, if you want a more paper-like experience, the Colorosoft beats the iPad.

Battery life is the one area where the Kindle Colorsoft clearly beats the iPad mini 8. Apple rates the iPad mini 8 as having up to 10 hours of battery life while surfing the web on Wi-Fi. Meanwhile, Amazon says the Colorsoft can last up to eight weeks on a single charge.

I’ve used the iPad mini 8 for nearly two weeks and have charged it about once every other day. Conversely, after almost three weeks, I still haven't charged the Kindle Colorsoft since I unboxed it. At the time of writing, it is at 60% battery life!

Kindles have always boasted long battery life since they are dedicated e-readers, unlike an iPad, which is a more demanding computing device. It’s no surprise that the Colorsoft can last much longer than the iPad mini 8.

This is another area where things don’t match one-to-one. You can read on both, but that’s about it on the Colorsoft, while the iPad mini 8 is basically a computer that can do so much more. To keep things fair though, I’ll focus on the respective Kindle ecosystem experiences the devices offer.

Naturally, the Kindle Colorsoft integrates seamlessly with the Kindle Store and Goodreads. I can easily purchase new books right on the Kindle and also upload my reading status to Goodreads.

UI-wise, the Home page shows all the books you’re currently reading or have read, a list of recommended books, and top Kindle Unlimited recommendations based on your reading history. You can also change the font, theme, and more within a book’s dedicated settings.

The iPadOS version of the Kindle app has all these features, except that the UI is arranged differently to fit an iPad’s screen size. One downside is that you can’t buy books directly in the Kindle app. To do that, you’ll have to open a web browser and log in to Amazon. That said, the Kindle app still syncs your reading status across Kindles and other devices.

One of Colorsoft’s major advantages over the iPad mini 8 is its truly distraction-free reading experience. You’ll never see pop-up notifications on a Kindle, whereas you’ll need to manually disable notifications on an iPad. It’s also much easier to wander off to another app on the iPad because everything is so easily accessible. On a Kindle, you can only read or browse for books.

Both the iPad mini 8 and Kindle Colorsoft are fantastic for reading. You can’t go wrong with either for that, but depending on what you’re looking for, one might suit you better than the other.

If you want an enhanced version of the classic Kindle experience, then the Kindle Colorsoft is the device to get. Its new design is superb, and colors look better than ever. Yes, it’s now the second-priciest Kindle after the Kindle Scribe, but there’s no denying Amazon delivered a more refined color e-reader.

Conversely, the iPad mini 8 impresses thanks to its premium design and mesmerizing OLED panel. Both black-and-white text and color images look absolutely stunning on its display. The lighter design also makes it more Kindle-like to hold. And if you want to do more than read, the iPad mini delivers strong performance for work, light gaming, and streaming videos.

Tripadvisor: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Tripadvisor is betting its marketing dollars on Viator, leaving legacy SEO-dependent channels to shrink as their traffic slows.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 6, 2026, Tripadvisor (TRIP -5.15%) announced a strategic pivot toward its experiences marketplace while shrinking its legacy hotel business. The operational shift reallocates key engineering talent to fuel faster-growing segments. Investors need to evaluate whether cost reductions in legacy lines can protect cash flow as paid search engine optimization traffic slows.

Three key takeaways from management's call stand out.

Tripadvisor's trimming 425 positions in its legacy hotels and other (H&O) segment to pare back fixed costs. Moving 150 of those engineering and data analytics roles to its experiences division helps fund growth where traveler demand's expanding.

Selling restaurant reservation platform TheFork gives management $685 million in net available cash to strengthen its balance sheet. Repaying long-term debt reduces interest expense and gives the company financial flexibility during ongoing strategic changes.

Persistent search engine optimization (SEO) challenges across legacy channels prompted management to stop splitting product development across separate sites. By concentrating spending on the online travel agency (OTA) Viator, the company aims to improve customer acquisition cost (CAC) and customer lifetime value (LTV) metrics.

Artificial intelligence integrations present a fresh distribution avenue across major platform partners, and management isn't hiding its satisfaction with early conversion rates. Tripadvisor relies on its user-generated content, supplier relationships, and brand trust to remain relevant as travel research shifts toward conversational tools.

Strategic execution focuses on debt reduction, cost discipline, and single-flywheel marketing investments to set a solid operational baseline for 2027.

iPhone 20 could roll the Action Button and Camera Control into a single button — and I'm very excited to see if that actually happens

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join the Tom's Guide Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Unlock the vast world of Apple news straight to your inbox. With coverage on everything from exciting product launches to essential software updates, this is your go-to source for the latest updates on all the best Apple content.

Our weekly newsletter is expertly crafted to immerse you in the world of streaming. Stay updated on the latest releases and our top recommendations across your favorite streaming platforms.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I've always been a little bit confused about the launch of Camera Control on the iPhone 16. It's one of those features that generally feels pretty redundant, especially since there are already better ways to quick launch the camera app that Apple hasn't bothered with yet. Well the good news is that the standalone Camera Control button could be on its way out, or at least it might be according to one leaker.

The leaker Instant Digital posted about this on their Weibo account, noting that the Camera Control and Action Buttons could be merged as soon as next year's iPhone 20. The new button will apparently launch the camera with a single tap, while press-and-hold will fire up whatever Action Button setting you have mapped. There's no mention of touch sensitivity, or any of the other Camera Control features that are currently available.

Instant Digital clarifies that this means the Action Button will be rolled into Camera Control, rather than the other way around. This presumably means the button will remain on the bottom right side of the phones, in the spot Camera Control currently occupies. That said, whichever way this might happen, I am all for it, because I've never liked Camera Control.

I've been using iPhones on and off for years, since before the Camera Control button was added to the iPhone 16 series two years ago. Apple marketed this button as a way to instantly access the camera with a single click and adjust camera features with your finger — rather than the touchscreen controls.

Apple didn't come up with the idea of a dedicated camera button, those have existed on smartphones for years, but mostly fell out of favor. Instead phones came up with better ways to access the camera with the existing button layout — including the well known "double tap" gesture on an Android phone's power button.

Admittedly Camera Control was meant for more. It was a way to access Visual Intelligence, until it got rolled into the camera app as "Siri Mode," and to adjust various different camera levels without having to poke around on the screen.

Though, in my experience with recent iPhones, that was never particularly appealing. On-screen controls always felt faster and simpler, especially since I could adjust things like zoom without taking my hands off the phone itself.

Sign up to the Tom's iGuide weekly newsletter with coverage on everything from product launches to software updates, this is your go-to source for the latest updates on all the best Apple content.

It's really not that difficult to do, and I've never felt the need to actually utilize the Camera Control button to the fullest extent of its capabilities. The older ways were easier and simpler to do, and didn't require much in the way of thinking first. Plus, the volume keys were (and still are) a valid way to activate the shutter for photos and video.

I've never fully understood why Apple felt the need to add that extra button to the side of the iPhone, when better alternatives were already being used. Especially after all the rumors that suggested Apple was trying to eliminate buttons altogether.

I'll admit, in the same way that I do not care for the Camera Control button, there are plenty of people who do. In fact some of them have already taken to social media sites like Reddit to discuss their own feelings about both the Action Button and Camera Control — many of them lamenting the fact that the current iterations may be lost.

I get it to an extent, but it still feels as though iPhones are getting a little too cluttered for my liking, especially on the smaller models. That's assuming you ignore all those rumors about a possible third button on the iPhone 17, which didn't come to pass because it would have been a step too far.

The features that are on offer aren't necessarily bad, but there are better ways to implement them. Combining Camera Control and the Action Button together is one good way of doing it. After all, the Action Button is triggered by a long press, while the Camera Control opens your camera with a single or double press. There's no reason why the two can't live in harmony as a single dual-purpose button.

Of course turning the Camera Control button into an Action Button will mean Siri Mode needs a new shortcut. At the moment, pressing and holding the Camera button launches the camera app in Apple's AI camera mode, just like how it launched Visual Intelligence before the release of iOS 27.

Likewise you won't be able to use the Action Button while the camera is open, since that action is already reserved for recording video — and nobody wants to get rid of that. I'm guessing that the emphasis on the claim that the Action Button will become part of Camera Control means the latter's touch-sensitive controls will stick around for people that actually use them.

Sure, it will take some getting used to, regardless of how old your current iPhone might be, but I think that it will end up being the more efficient option in the long run. I just hope they pick a more sensible spot, because the bottom right side of an iPhone doesn't really scream "Action" to me.

The one thing that has to be made very clear is that this is an unverified rumor. Instant Digital doesn't have a bad reputation, but that doesn't mean they have a 100% track record, and we're still pretty early in the iPhone 20 development cycle. So, in other words, there's no guarantee that Apple will actually make this change with the iPhone 20.

Especially when you remember all the other Camera Control rumors that never came true, and the fact the iPhone 20 is also rumored to swap out buttons for haptic sensors. If every phone rumor we heard turned out to be true, these devices would be a complete mess.

Still, I would quite like to see this happen. The Action Button is useful, and I don't want to see that go away, but there are plenty of people who have grown to like the Camera Control button too — even if I don't really care for it myself. If you're going to keep both, you might as well merge them into a single, easy to use button.

At the very least it's going to look and feel neater than plastering the iPhone with more buttons than a keyboard.

The Evolution Of Airplane Window Design

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Boarding a plane today means stepping into a highly structured, carefully designed world. Regardless of whether we are crammed into the last row of economy or stretching out in a first class suite, our human eyes always instinctively gravitate toward the exact same feature: the window. That small, oval piece of multi-layered acrylic and glass is our only physical connection to the absolute miracle of hurtling through the stratosphere at six hundred miles per hour.

As an industrial design engineer who spends an unhealthy amount of time thinking about cabin interiors, I can tell you that the window is the most psychologically vital component of the passenger experience. In this article, we will examine the fascinating evolution of the airplane window, why it looks the way it does, and why the aviation industry's obsession with replacing it with 'digital windows' is almost guaranteed to be an absolute disaster for how we feel when we fly.

To understand why the future of airplane windows is so concerning, you have to look at how much their engineering has evolved to keep us safe in the past. During the early days of pressurized jet travel in the 1950s, aircraft manufacturers were learning hard lessons about aerospace physics. Early commercial jets featured large, square windows that looked beautiful but structurally compromised the fuselage, leading to catastrophic failures.

Today, the industry is a highly competitive, data-driven machine built on optimizing weight, fuel burn, and aerodynamic efficiency. Every actual hole cut into the side of an aircraft requires heavy structural reinforcement, which adds weight and burns more jet fuel. While we might love the view, airlines and manufacturers look at windows as heavy, expensive liabilities.

This tension between structural efficiency and human psychology is pushing us toward a future of screen-based, digital windows. This is a modern approach that, while brilliant on a spreadsheet, entirely misses what human beings actually need to survive twelve hours in a metal tube. The shape of the modern airplane window is not a stylistic choice made by an interior decorator: it is an engineering mandate born from tragedy.

When the de Havilland Comet, the world's first commercial jetliner, took to the skies in the early 1950s, it featured large, square windows that provided passengers with glorious, panoramic views. However, engineers soon discovered a terrifying flaw in this design. When an aircraft cabin is repeatedly pressurized and depressurized during flight cycles, the stress of that expansion concentrates heavily on the sharp 90-degree corners of square windows.

This phenomenon, known as metal fatigue, led to microscopic cracks that eventually caused the aircraft to break apart mid-flight. The immediate solution was to eliminate the corners entirely, leading to the ubiquitous round and oval windows we see on every commercial jet today. By using a rounded shape, the stress of the cabin pressure flows smoothly around the window frame rather than building up in a single spot. It is a brilliant, life-saving piece of aerospace engineering.

However, creating these reinforced oval holes in a cylindrical aluminium or composite fuselage is incredibly complex. The window assemblies are heavy, requiring thick, multi-pane acrylic layers to withstand freezing temperatures, immense pressure differentials, and the occasional bird strike, all while being perfectly sealed.

This brings us to a massive, silent frustration for the modern passenger: the dreaded 'windowless' window seat. Because cutting holes in the airframe is so dictated by the structural ribs of the Original Equipment Manufacturer (like Boeing, Airbus or Embraer), the windows are placed at fixed, mathematically rigid intervals. But airlines want to squeeze as many seats into the plane as possible.

Using the modern seat-track system on the floor, airlines tend to compress the seat pitch with zero regard for where the actual windows are. This is why you often pay extra for a window seat, only to spend a six-hour flight staring at a blank, curved plastic wall where the seat rows have completely fallen out of sync with the aircraft's architecture.

In the last decade, we witnessed the first major disruption to the airplane window in half a century: the removal of the physical plastic window shade. When Boeing introduced the 787 Dreamliner in 2009, they debuted massive, electronically dimmable windows. Airbus, a while later in 2022, followed suit on the A350.

Instead of pulling down a piece of plastic to block out the sun, passengers press a button, and an electrical current passes through a special electrochromic gel sandwiched between the windowpanes, slowly turning the glass from fully transparent to a deep, dark blue. From an industrial design standpoint, this is a remarkable piece of technology.

It eliminates the mechanical failure of plastic shades snapping off their tracks or breaking, reduces cabin weight, and allows passengers to look outside without letting blinding, harsh sunlight flood the cabin and ruin the contrast of everyone's IFE screens. This feels like magic the first time you use it.

However, it also introduced a subtle, highly controversial shift in cabin dynamics: it transferred environmental control from the passenger to the cabin crew. In my opinion, this is not bad at all, since it lets airlines control the circadian rhythm of the majority of their guests and takes away the possibility of a single passenger disturbing the rest and sleep of a whole cabin of passengers just because they want to see the view.

This shift, however, profoundly affects the passenger experience. With electrochromic windows, the flight crew has a master override switch in the galley. On a daytime flight across the Atlantic, the crew can lock every window in the cabin to the darkest setting to force a sleeping environment, effectively trapping passengers in artificial twilight.

While this is great for minimizing jet lag and keeping the cabin quiet, it is deeply frustrating for the aviation geek who wants to see Greenland from 38,000 feet. Giving the passenger a personal window but electronically denying them the ability to look out of it perfectly illustrates the modern airline's desire to manage passenger behavior through cabin design.

If dimmable glass was a big evolution, the next big idea circulating in aerospace design circles is a total revolution: the complete elimination of physical passenger windows, replaced entirely by high-definition 'digital windows' (the fancy name for a simple screen) that show a live video feed from external cameras. We have already seen the first iterations of this in the industry.

Indeed, Emirates famously installed 'virtual windows' in the middle-aisle first class Game-changer Suites on its Boeing 777s. These are highly advanced, ultra-high-definition screens shaped exactly like airplane windows. They display a real-time feed from fiber-optic cameras mounted on the exterior of the aircraft, and are probably the only justifiable application (and a genius one indeed), to have digital window screens on an aircraft.

To an airline accountant or an aerospace engineer focused on sustainability and cost savings, a windowless fuselage is like the holy grail. If you remove all the windows, you can build a perfectly smooth, uninterrupted metallic or carbon-fiber tube. The aircraft becomes significantly lighter, massively more aerodynamic, and burns drastically less fuel. Furthermore, supersonic jet concepts, which are highly sensitive to drag and structural integrity, frequently feature windowless cabins as a baseline requirement.

Airlines would save millions on fuel and give the passenger a 4K screen that looks just as good as the real thing. However, for the passenger experience and well-being, that just doesn't cut it, as a screen is fundamentally not a window. When you look out of a real airplane window, your eyes focus on infinity. As you move your head, your perspective of the clouds and the ground shifts, a geometric phenomenon known as parallax.

A digital window, no matter how high the resolution, is a flat, two-dimensional light-emitting surface placed two feet away from your face. The camera on the outside of the plane captures one single perspective, and no matter how you move your head in your seat, the image on the screen remains static. Your brain instantly recognizes this physical disconnect, shattering the illusion of transparency.

From booking to upgrades to dining, Emirates' first class cabin defines the carrier's ethos of "Fly Better."

The most severe consequence of implementing digital windows across an entire cabin is the immediate psychological toll it will take on the passengers. Humans are biologically hardwired to seek natural light and spatial awareness, especially when placed in confined, moving, and vibrating environments like the interior of a car, train, or aircraft. The physical window is the ultimate antidote to claustrophobia.

It proves to our panicked subconscious that there is a vast, open world just on the other side of that glass oval. Replacing that organic connection with a glowing LCD screen turns the aircraft from a flying ship into a flying submarine. Beyond claustrophobia, digital windows will wreak havoc on our circadian rhythms.

Natural sunlight contains a complex, full-spectrum biological signal that tells our brains what time of day it is, helping us adjust to new time zones. A digital screen, conversely, is just a matrix of red, green, and blue pixels heavily biased toward the blue light spectrum. Staring at a high-definition monitor disguised as a window for a 14-hour flight will increase eye strain, induce fatigue, and confuse our internal clocks.

We are already surrounded by screens, such as the in-flight entertainment screens, our phones, and our tablets, so turning the actual architecture of the cabin into yet another screen will become a sensory overload that steps away from the ultimate passenger experience. Then there is the very real issue of motion sickness. The human vestibular system (the inner ear that controls balance) is highly sensitive to mismatched inputs. When an aircraft banks, turns, or hits turbulence, your inner ear feels the motion.

If you are looking out a real window, your eyes see the horizon tilt, and your brain reconciles the two inputs perfectly. With a digital window, any microscopic latency, also known as digital delay, in the camera feed, or any distortion in the screen's refresh rate, means your eyes and your inner ear will fall out of sync. This sensory mismatch is the exact recipe for aggressive, cabin-wide motion sickness, headache, and nausea, which airlines want to avoid at all costs when transporting hundreds of paying passengers.

As an engineer, one of the first questions I always ask myself when evaluating a new cabin product is what happens when it breaks? Because everything on an airplane breaks, eventually. Liquid gets spilled, heavy bags are dropped, and complex electrical systems degrade over time due to the extreme vibrations of flight.

A traditional acrylic window is incredibly robust: even if the inner protective plastic gets scratched by a passenger's ring, the window still functions perfectly. It still lets light in, and you can still see outside. A digital window, however, introduces a massive, unnecessary layer of technological fragility to the core architecture of the cabin.

What happens when the fiber-optic camera on the outside of the fuselage is obscured by ice, insect impacts, or heavy rain? The passenger is left staring at a blurry, pixelated mess. What happens when the screen panel inside the cabin suffers a backlight failure or a dead pixel matrix?

Instead of a view of the Alps, the passenger who paid extra for a window seat is forced to sit next to a glowing blue screen, or worse, a completely black, dead monitor for an ultra-long-haul flight. This creates an absolute nightmare for in-flight service recovery. If a passenger's in-flight entertainment screen breaks today, a good flight attendant can apologize, offer them an iPad, or give them some frequent flyer miles to make up for it.

However, if the actual window breaks, and the passenger is plunged into claustrophobic darkness while their window-mates have working screens, there is no iPad that can fix that anxiety. The maintenance burden on the airlines not only would be astronomical, but would also require specialized technicians to ground the aircraft simply to fix or replace a glitchy screen on the sidewall. This would entirely defeat the operational cost savings that the windowless fuselage was supposed to provide in the first place.

When we take a step back and look at the trajectory of cabin design, it becomes clear that the push for digital windows is a classic case of engineering for the spreadsheet rather than engineering for the human. Yes, a windowless fuselage is lighter. Yes, it burns less fuel. However, an airplane is not a cargo drone: it is a vehicle designed to transport living, breathing, anxious, and sometimes claustrophobic human beings across the globe.

The physical airplane window is the last remaining organic connection we have to the outside world when we fly. It is the only thing that proves we are suspended in the heavens, participating in the greatest technological achievement of the 20th century, rather than just sitting in a loud and vibrating waiting room.

The commercial aviation industry, and especially widebody aircraft manufacturers such as Airbus and Boeing, must remember that passenger comfort is not just about the density of the seat cushion, the width of the armrest, or having some fancy mood lighting. Rather, it is deeply rooted in psychology and spatial awareness.

Stripping away the natural light, the true horizon, and the infinite focal depth of a real window, only to replace it with a simulated, two-dimensional broadcast, is a profound downgrade to the passenger experience, as it trades our mental well-being for a marginal increase in aerodynamic efficiency and profit. As we look toward the next generation of aircraft design, we have to ask ourselves where we draw the line between structural optimization and human dignity.

Furthermore, we should place a red line for decision-makers in the industry to ensure they pursue true innovations that really elevate the passenger experience, rather than degrade it even further. If we are willing to accept artificial screens disguised as windows simply to save some pounds of jet fuel per flight and manufacturing costs, how far are we willing to let the passenger experience decay before we realize that looking at a video of the sky won't ever replace the magic of being in it?

Cathie Wood 'always feels better when the market is worried' — here's why

📅 2026-11-08  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ark Invest CEO Cathie Wood is shrugging off this week's market jitters, telling CNBC on Friday that concerns among investors actually reinforce her optimism that further gains lie ahead.

U.S. equities sold off sharply on Thursday, reversing the initial rally seen in the wake of the midterm elections. AI-related stocks led the rout, sending the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite into correction territory. On Friday, tech stocks staged a tentative recovery.

Much of the sell-off on Thursday was driven by concerns that a Democrat-led Congress would lead to a slowdown in data center construction. But concerns about artificial intelligence – particularly when it comes to capital expenditure, cash flow, debt and circular financing – have sparked multiple bouts of volatility over the course of this year.

Wood, whose firm manages various funds focused on tech and innovation, told CNBC there was a "wall of worry" pervading financial markets right now. But her decades of experience as an investor give her a somewhat unconventional view of the state of play.

"I experienced the tech and telecom bubble, and you know there really wasn't much worry about it," she said. "It was the 80s and the 90s, and it was a bull market, and people were just assuming this would go to the moon, that is not how this [the AI trade] has evolved."

She explained that "from the beginning," many investors have been concerned about how much firms are spending on AI development, how rapidly the spending is taking place, and how the hyperscalers have moved from extreme levels of positive free cash flow to negative free cash flow.

"So it's been unsettling the markets for quite some time," she said. "I always feel better when the market is worried, because that tends to suggest a more durable bull market, and I don't think we've derailed this bull market."

In 2025, her flagship Ark Innovation ETF gained more than 35%, far outperforming the S&P 500's 16.4% rise and the Nasdaq Composite's 20.5% rally.

Asked on Friday whether markets were anywhere near the end of the current bull run, Wood replied: "I don't think so."

"I think we're in the middle of a technology revolution," she said, pointing to the development of technologies like robotaxis and AI agents.

One thing that did risk becoming a "showstopper," Wood noted, was the rising cost of capital.

Interest rates and bond yields have risen sharply this year as the U.S.-Iran war pushes inflation higher across the globe. Yields on corporate bonds have been rising too, with creditors demanding greater compensation for lending to companies.

"This correction is partly caused by what's going on in the bond markets," Wood said on Friday.

"I had been saying for quite some time that the wall of worry has been built by senior leaders in the financial world who were young during the bubble," she said.

"It's the elder statesmen and women of the industry who are keeping this wall of worry in place, and that's not bad. Now you can add the bond investor to that. Bond investors in the old days weren't necessary for technology. It was software. It was low fixed cost. It was viral apps, very high margins. [AI] is high fixed cost, so bond investors are experiencing or taking a journey into the technology world, really that they haven't very much before."

But she noted that AI hyperscalers were somewhat insulated by opportunities for growth.

"If a company thinks that taking on debt is going to accelerate its momentum, meaning its growth rate, it hardly matters what happens to interest rates for those companies," Wood explained.

Wood said her advice to investors in the current volatile environment was to identify their high-conviction positions and double down on them as the market shifts.

"When you see a rally in a volatile environment, take some profits. When you see a sell-off, you'll have the psychological wherewithal if you have taken profits to move back in," she said.

"That's what we do. So what we do during periods like this is concentrate towards our highest conviction names. So that's what we've been doing in the last week or so. Our SpaceX position has gone up. If anything, the controversy around data centers on Earth is giving data centers in space a bigger opportunity."

In 2027, Wood said she expects the equity rally to continue.

"This is two steps forward, one step back," she said of the post-election turbulence. "The revolution will become clearer and clearer next year, and I think the market will do well."

Is Broadcom a Buy After Falling 9% in One Day?

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The recent drop in Broadcom stock does not align with its fundamentals.

Broadcom (AVGO +0.74%) dropped by 9% as artificial intelligence (AI) fears continue to compound. Financing risks and questions about the ROI on AI investments dominated October, and midterm results are the new key item in November.

The growth stock was once approaching $500 and is now under $300 at the time of this writing. However, this drop does not correlate with the status of Broadcom's fundamentals. The dip looks like a good buying opportunity for long-term investors.

It's always good to look at a company's recent earnings results and guidance before making a decision regarding its stock. In this case, Broadcom delivered superb results.

Revenue jumped by 48% year-over-year, with AI semiconductor revenue doing the heavy lifting. That part of the business was up by 143% year-over-year and represented almost half of total sales.

Furthermore, Broadcom guided for AI semiconductor revenue to more than triple in its fiscal 2026 third quarter, which the company will report in early December. The momentum is not limited to 2026. The chipmaker is on record anticipating $115 billion in AI semiconductor sales in fiscal 2027, and that number is expected to double to $230 billion in its fiscal 2028.

Broadcom also expects more than $30 in earnings per share (EPS) in its fiscal 2028. That implies a forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio below 10 at current levels. The fact that Broadcom guided for fiscal 2028 shows that chip sales are still heating up.

Democrat victories in the midterm do not mean that the AI buildout is canceled. Regulations may get stricter, and future tax breaks may be less generous, but it's extremely unlikely that Democrats order a complete halt for the entire industry.

The AI buildout isn't just producing data centers. It's also creating jobs, and data center developers are investing directly into communities.

Even if Democrats clamp down on data centers harder than expected, especially in Texas, tech giants have been investing in international data centers for years. For example, Alphabet recently announced a $15 billion investment in a Malaysian data center.

Hyperscalers have been more than happy to buy AI chips, including the ones that Broadcom creates. While Nvidia is the undisputed leader in the GPU industry, Broadcom has established itself as the firm leader in the Application-Specific Integrated Circuit (ASIC) industry.

Whether large data centers are primarily built in the U.S. or international countries in the future remains anyone's guess, but all of those facilities will need chips. Tech spending isn't going down and continues to trend upward. Broadcom remains well-positioned in the AI boom despite the ongoing correction.

Marc Guberti has positions in Broadcom. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet, Broadcom, and Nvidia. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

A town of 6,600 people took on a $900 million AI data center — and won. Here's how they did it.

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join the Tom's Guide Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.
