Arista’s record quarter shows the network has become central to AI

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

GUEST COLUMN by Zeus Kerravala

The artificial intelligence buildout is well underway, with much of the focus on silicon and compute hardware. The network is treated as plumbing, something you connect after the important decisions have been made. Arista Networks Inc.’s strong third-quarter results signaled greater awareness of the network’s critical role, and Arista is well-positioned to benefit from this secular trend.

Arista reported third-quarter revenue of $3.66 billion, up 58.5% year over year and 20.5% sequentially. That is the fastest growth in the company’s history as a public company and well ahead of its $3.3 billion guide. Adjusted, non-GAAP earnings per share were $1.20, up from 77 cents a year ago. Gross margin was 64%, operating margin was 49.1%, and cash totaled $14.4 billion with no debt.

Management raised full-year 2026 guidance for the fourth time this year to about $13.3 billion, representing 48% growth. Fourth-quarter revenue is expected to come in between $3.9 billion and $4 billion. Arista also gave a first look at 2027, calling for 38% to 40% growth to about $18.5 billion. Within that, AI networking is expected to double from $3.5 billion to about $7 billion, and campus is expected to grow 60% to about $2 billion. The shares rose about 12% in after-hours trading, moderating to a 5% rise today.

Though investors will always gravitate toward the numbers as proof points, the commentary by Chief Executive Jayshree Ullal (pictured) was equally important. “We believe the industry is shifting from a compute-centric vertical stack to a holistic network-centric approach,” she said.

I agree, and the reason is economics. In large clusters, the network often determines how productive the AI factory is. When packets drop or a link fails, expensive accelerators sit idle, and inference makes the problem worse. Ullal described the move from “highly centralized training compute to disaggregated and distributed inference,” with prefill, decode, storage and KV cache spread across resources. “A well-designed network is becoming a foundational cornerstone for optimal token efficiency,” she said.

Buyers are getting it. Boards and chief financial officers now measure AI by cost per token and accelerator utilization, both of which depend on the network. A 5% gain in GPU utilization across a multigigawatt build is worth far more than the switches that delivered it. Once you understand that math, the network shifts from a commodity to a performance decision.

This awareness benefits every networking vendor, but it disproportionately benefits Arista for three reasons.

The first is accelerator diversity. Three years ago, Arista talked about four or five AI customers running 50,000 to 100,000 Nvidia Corp. graphics processing units, often on InfiniBand. Today, it has more than 100 Etherlink customers and expects more than 10 gigawatts of deployment across 10 or more types of accelerators by the end of 2027.

“In 2026, we are in a super cycle of AI,” Ullal said. “What a stark inflection in just three years.” Nvidia is a formidable networking competitor, and its Spectrum-X Ethernet has won over hyperscalers such as Meta Platforms Inc. and Oracle Corp. But Nvidia’s networking is optimized around its own full stack, and as customers bring Advanced Micro Devices Inc., Microsoft Corp.’s Maia, Meta MTIA, Qualcomm Corp., and Cerebras  Inc. silicon into production, many will prefer a network supplier that isn’t also selling a competing accelerator.

Every new accelerator expands the share of AI networking open to an independent Ethernet vendor, and Arista leads that category. Co-design work with Cerebras and Maia means Arista is now involved in system design rather than added afterward.

The second is scale-across. Power limits are forcing AI capacity to be spread across buildings and regions, and connecting those sites requires deep buffers, large routing tables and very high reliability. Ullal said scale-across accounted for about 30% of AI demand this year and should rise in 2027, with the 7800 platform as the anchor. “There’s just no room for error when you’re connecting millions of XPUs,” she said. This is where Arista’s routing and EOS software experience matter most.

The third is scale-up. Arista showcased rack-scale Ethernet designs at OCP, built on the Ethernet for Scale-Up Networking or ESUN specification, with trials in 2027 and production in 2028. Ullal compared it to the InfiniBand transition: Arista expects to move from “staring at proprietary UALink or NVLink” to “of course, it’s going to be ESUN.” If that happens, Arista’s market expands inside the rack, a segment it has never served.

Security strengthens the argument. Chief Technology Officer Ken Duda said Arista is working with Anthropic PBC, Google LLC and OpenAI Group PBC to integrate AI-driven vulnerability discovery into its software pipeline, including through Project Glasswing. As AI finds flaws faster, an operating system with process isolation and hitless patching becomes an advantage.

Ullal drew the contrast sharply: “Everybody else is dealing with a game of Whac-A-Mole where they’ve got thousands of vulnerabilities and no AVA can possibly keep track of it.” President and Chief Operating Officer Todd Nightingale said this “Glasswing and Mythos effect” is a major reason campus is growing so far ahead of the market.

The stock had fallen more than 20% in the month before the report, according to Chartmill, and analysts raised several concerns.

Deferred revenue. Total deferred revenue was flat at about $6.8 billion, which some interpret as a sign of slowing demand. CFO Chantelle Breithaupt said the balance had “the largest churn in and out” yet stayed flat while the company delivered 58.5% growth, and that the year-end balance will be higher than last year. Ullal said parsing it that way turns demand into “a physics problem,” adding, “We’re just in the best position we’ve been in a long, long time in my 18-year career at Arista.”

Spending and margins. Research and development jumped to $408.5 million from $278.1 million. Duda attributed the increase to hyperscaler prototypes and liquid cooling, and Breithaupt kept the long-term R&D range at 9% to 11% of revenue. Gross margin dipped from 65.2% a year ago, but Ullal held the long-term range at 62% to 64%, including for scale-up products.

Supply and visibility. Purchase commitments rose to $11.4 billion, and inventory to $3.2 billion. Multiyear agreements now cover silicon, circuit boards and memory. Regarding visibility, Ullal said that although Arista has two quarters of order visibility, “we have two years of work behind it.”

For several years, the debate over Arista has centered on whether Ethernet could displace InfiniBand. While InfiniBand will continue to play a key role in AI buildouts, Ethernet has much broader applicability, and its growth will outpace InfiniBand.

The new question is how much of the AI system the network will define, and this quarter suggests the answer is more than most expected. As accelerators multiply, inference spreads and AI-driven threats grow, value moves toward the layer that connects and protects everything. That is Arista’s layer.

Sebastien Naji of William Blair expressed a similar view in his earnings note. “As networking becomes a more important lever for improving performance and token output in AI data centers, Arista is well positioned to capture a growing share of spending across the most demanding AI workloads,” he wrote. “Combined with continued share gains at enterprises and neoclouds, we believe Arista’s expanding addressable market and competitive positioning support further upside in the coming quarters. We continue to view Arista as a core AI infrastructure holding and reiterate our Outperform rating.”

The biggest risk to Arista isn’t deferred revenue or market demand; it’s whether it can keep executing as crisply as it has as it gets bigger and demand accelerates But execution is something the company has excelled at under Jayshree Ullal.

Zeus Kerravala is a principal analyst at ZK Research, a division of Kerravala Consulting. He wrote this article for SiliconANGLE.

Democratic sweep of Congress forces crypto industry to rethink Washington strategy

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

As results rolled in, crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto said 19 pro-crypto lawmakers in the Senate had been elected and 226 in the House as Democrats took control of the House and Senate.

After two years of a pro-crypto, pro-innovation climate in Washington, the digital asset industry faces a sharp political jolt as Democrats take control of both chambers of Congress, forcing the sector to seek new allies within a party that has been more divided on crypto.

As results rolled in on Tuesday, crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto said 19 pro-crypto lawmakers in the Senate had been elected and 226 in the House as Democrats took control of the House and Senate. Eight anti-crypto Senators were also elected alongside 106 in the House.

Following the results, some in the crypto industry have taken the position that crypto is bipartisan and that lawmakers on both sides of the aisle are pro-digital assets. But a Democratic sweep means more oversight, and there is close to no chance of the crypto industry getting legislation it wants on the table.

"Nobody has their head in the sand," said Kara Calvert, vice president of U.S. policy at Coinbase, in a post on X. "There will be headwinds for any bipartisan legislation next year, but crypto is 100% bipartisan."

Many of the new members in Congress are pro-crypto, said Kevin Wysocki, head of policy at Anchorage Digital.

"The younger members are more used to this technology, are more open to new technology that maybe rivals some of the existing technology," he said in an interview with The Block on Wednesday. "It's very bullish, I think, for crypto overall, so I think that's one of the great things from what happened yesterday, and I think it's only going to get better over time, regardless of who's in power, Republicans or Democrats."

However, one of the biggest races the crypto industry had its eyes on, and money on, was the Ohio Senate race between former Democratic Sen. Sherrod Brown and SWC-endorsed Republican Sen. Jon Husted. The crypto political action committee spent tens of millions of dollars on that race against Brown, but ultimately lost when Brown defeated Husted later in the night.

Brown was previously Senate Banking Committee Chair, which has jurisdiction over digital assets and has been viewed as critical of crypto. He could have a leading role in oversight and regulation of the industry. And since he won in a special election to fill Vice President JD Vance's Senate term, that means Brown could return to the Senate as early as Dec. 15.

Brown's return is significant, Wysocki said. When he was chair of the Senate Banking Committee, Brown and his Senate staff were open to constructive conversations about crypto, he said.

Pro-crypto political action committees reportedly spent over $60 million alone in the Senate race in Ohio against Brown. On its own, crypto PAC Fairshake raised $106.2 million, according to Americans for Financial Reform. And crypto PACs overall spent $170 million on the midterms, according to Fortune, with millions going to Republican Sen. Pete Ricketts in Nebraska.

Fairshake spent $154.5 million in both primaries and the general election, with over $13.5 million going to Ricketts.

"Fairshake played a meaningful role in winning competitive races across the country in 2026 and we will do it again in 2028," said Geoff Vetter, spokesperson for Fairshake, in an emailed statement to The Block. "Newly elected and current members will have a choice: protect consumers and American jobs or fight progress. We will be watching and '28 is a new map."

One crypto industry source alluded that PACs and trade associations will have to "recalibrate" and need to "double down on being bipartisan."

Specifically on Fairshake, the person criticized funds being used on Brown's race.

"What did we ultimately get out of that?" they said. "Where could that money have been better spent? To be perfectly honest with you, I think it could have been better spent on shoe-leather lobbying."

That money could have been spent to lobby lawmakers on the Clarity Act, broad crypto legislation that passed the House, but got stuck in the Senate in September, they said.

"You spend a fraction of it, and you could have effectively hired all of DC to lobby on Clarity," they added.

Focus will turn to taxes and security and hacks, Anchorage's Wysocki said. The election, too, comes just days after the largest crypto hack on record of Binance for over $6 billion.

"Congress is going to be asking those questions, like how do we make security at our financial institutions stronger?" Wysocki said.

The Clarity Act that passed out of the House last year and stalled in the Senate a few months ago is unlikely to advance over the next two years given Brown's win, said investment bank TD Cowen.

Oversight will also come into view, including investigations into Trump-family-run World Liberty Financial, said TD Cowen’s Washington Research Group, led by managing director Jaret Seiberg.

"Democrats will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements, including World Liberty Financial," Seiberg wrote in a note on Wednesday. "We believe the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

Where voters stand on state ballot measures, from abortion to LGBTQ rights to guns

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Voters weighed in on 146 statewide ballot measures across dozens of states. Mario Tama/Getty Images hide caption

Tuesday's midterm elections were a huge sweep for Democrats, among other takeaways.

And across 39 states — plus Washington, D.C. and the Northern Mariana Islands — voters weighed in on 146 statewide ballot measures spanning issues from taxes to transgender rights to traffic cameras to data centers and AI.

Three states moved to preserve abortion access

The U.S. Supreme Court overturned Roe v. Wade in 2022, leaving the legality and regulation of abortion up to each state. In subsequent elections, over a dozen states have put access to abortion on their ballots — and overwhelmingly moved to secure it.

This year, four states considered abortion measures. Three passed, while Idaho voted to preserve the state's abortion ban from 2022.

In Missouri, voters rejected a Republican-led amendment that would have banned abortion with few exceptions. The move affirms support for abortion just two years after voters mobilized to reject Missouri's ban, which was one of the strictest in the country.

Nevada residents voted to enshrine abortion up to the point of viability in their constitution. They did so for the second election in a row, since state law requires a proposed constitutional amendment to pass twice.

Virginians voted to create a "fundamental right to reproductive freedom" in their constitution. That includes the right to an abortion during the first two trimesters (and afterward in certain cases), as well as the right to contraception, fertility, postpartum care and more.

Many states considered changes to how they run their elections.

It was too early to call Wednesday afternoon whether voters had approved some of the more far-reaching changes, in Alaska and Arizona.

Other states — including Nevada, North Carolina and Ohio — passed measures toughening identification requirements for voters, while California voted against such an effort.

Several states — including Alaska, Arkansas, Kansas, South Dakota and West Virginia — approved measures reinforcing that U.S. citizenship is required to vote in their elections.

Redistricting was also top of mind for voters. President Trump kicked off a redistricting race earlier this year by urging Republican-led states to redraw their districts ahead of the midterms. Republicans and Democrats in multiple states did so. But many of the districts altered to boost GOP prospects ultimately went to Democratic candidates.

Missouri voters rejected a Republican-friendly congressional map, while Maryland voters passed a redistricting amendment that could pave the way for a Democrat-leaning map in 2028.

Virginians passed a measure automatically restoring voting rights to anyone convicted of a felony once they have completed their sentence.

Virginians also passed a constitutional amendment officially repealing its ban on gay marriage and adding language confirming that the commonwealth will recognize marriage between two adults regardless of their sex, gender or race.

Virginia's ban has been moot since the Supreme Court made same-sex marriage legal nationwide in 2015, but this measure is seen as a safeguard in case Obergefell v. Hodges gets reversed (as some have feared in the wake of the court's overturning of Roe v. Wade). Other states have made similar moves, including Colorado and California in 2024.

Vermont overwhelmingly voted to add an amendment to its constitution guaranteeing equal protection under the law "race, ethnicity, sex, religion, disability, sexual orientation, gender identity, gender expression, or national origin."

A number of states considered measures restricting transgender kids' access to school sports teams and gender-affirming care, with Colorado passing both. A Washington state measure to ban trans students from competing in girls' school sports was still leading on Wednesday.

Arizona passed a proposal requiring schools and athletic associations to restrict students, athletes and employees from using facilities — including restrooms, locker rooms, and other private spaces — that are not designated for their sex assigned at birth. Arizona law already bans trans girls from playing on school sports teams designated for girls, and this measure would extend that prohibition to recreational organizations like Little League.

One of the most high-profile ballot measures asked Floridians to weigh cutting property taxes for homeowners, which critics say would take billions of dollars in local government revenue away from much-needed public services like food banks, libraries, transportation and infrastructure. Voters overwhelmingly passed the amendment.

In California, voters pondered whether to approve a one-off 5% tax on individuals with more than $1 billion in assets, with most of the money raised going toward health care services. The fight against that ballot measure was one of the most expensive in the state's history. The contest is too close to call as of Wednesday afternoon.

Tennessee voters approved a constitutional amendment prohibiting its government from levying a statewide property tax, which it has not collected since 1949. Arizona voters passed a measure that bans cities and towns from raising taxes on groceries until July 2027. North Carolinians approved an amendment to cap the maximum personal income tax at 3.5%.

Washington voters considered whether to repeal an income tax on individuals and households earning more than $1 million, which is scheduled to take effect in 2028. Early results indicate they want to keep that tax.

A polar-opposite pair of gun ballot measures

Massachusetts voted to keep a 2024 law tightening firearm regulations. The law made Massachusetts gun control measures among the strictest in the nation, as it also included requiring "ghost guns" to have a serial number, broadened the definition of "assault-style firearm" and expanded the state's red flag laws.

Meanwhile, in Idaho, a ballot measure asked voters for help choosing the official state gun from six different brands and models. The Winchester Model 1873 — aka the "Gun that Won the West" — leads in the polls as of Wednesday, though the state legislature would need to pass a separate law to make the winner official.

The ballot initiatives about data centers

At least 38 jurisdictions voted on the sprawling facilities needed to power AI, and Tuesday revealed how many voters dislike them. Jurisdictions in California, Florida, Illinois, Kansas, Maine, Nevada, New Jersey, North Dakota, Ohio, and Wisconsin voted to restrict data centers.

Of these, at least 12 localities voted to restrict the size of data centers, while six others voted to halt development and ban construction of all facilities, according to Data Centers on the Ballot.

The one community that supported data centers is in Harwood, North Dakota, where 75% of voters did not want to place restrictions on industrial projects, including data centers.

Ohio, one of the frontline states on the issue, had 21 ballot measures mentioning data centers. Voters there moved to restrict the AI facilities: Trenton, Ohio voted to ban data centers over 25 megawatts, while Hubbard moved to ban construction of data centers of any size, including converting existing buildings. Ohio voters also rejected rezoning areas from rural residential to light industrial for data center usage.

Candidates spent up to $40 million on ads about data centers this election cycle. They were a contentious issue in many statewide elections, as 77% of Americans say they are concerned about data centers raising electricity costs, according to a June Reuters/Ipsos poll. The poll also showed two-thirds of Democrats and half of Republicans said they'd oppose a data center in their community.

Alabama voters passed ballot measures requiring local school boards to adopt policies directing public K-12 schools to recite the Pledge of Allegiance and, separately, play or perform the first stanza of "The Star-Spangled Banner" at least once a week during school hours.

In Arizona, voters passed a measure banning the state from installing and using photo enforcement systems (like speed and red-light cameras) to catch people who violate certain traffic ordinances.

Residents of Washington, D.C. had just one ballot initiative to consider: banning the controversial duck or goose liver dish known as foie gras. The debate went viral in recent days, prompting a slew of Halloween costumes, a four-course restaurant tasting menu and public appearances by a real duck named Olivia. D.C. voted overwhelmingly to prohibit the force-feeding of birds to enlarge their livers, and the sale of the delicacy itself. Across the country, voters in Denver approved a similar measure.

Critical Rails Bug Threatens to Derail Vulnerable Servers

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An attacker could write arbitrary files and read sensitive files via images uploaded to the Rails Active Storage ImageMagick integration, possibly positioning a threat actor for lateral movement.

A critical vulnerability in Rails could send impacted Web servers off the tracks.

Rails, or Ruby on Rails, is a Web application framework built on Ruby. The framework's Active Storage component handles file uploads and can use the ImageMagick image processing suite to analyze uploaded images. And unfortunately, a flaw enables attackers to write arbitrary files to a Web server via ImageMagick.

The vulnerability (which is tracked as GHSA-f3w6-w445-fwh9) is critical, with a CVSS score of 9.8, and is considered so severe because an attacker could upload a crafted image file to write arbitrary files, read sensitive files, access secrets such as secret_key_base files, external system credentials, database passwords, and third-party tokens. These secrets could then be used for lateral movement or remote code execution into said external systems.

As a GitHub advisory published by the Rails maintainers explains, "A Rails application that processes attachments with ImageMagick may allow an unauthenticated attacker to write arbitrary files on the server and, when attachments are stored with the Disk service, to read arbitrary files readable by the application process."

Related:Undead Software Projects Continue to Raise Security Risks

The vulnerability exists because ImageMagick doesn't necessarily decide how to process a file (i.e., which coder it uses) based on the filename or extension, but rather on its contents. This means an attacker can upload an image file named "maliciousfile.jpeg" but contains content that causes ImageMagick to invoke a different coder. This can potentially allow arbitrary file reads or writes on the server.

Vulnerable versions of Active Storage include those 8.0 versions prior to 8.0.5.2 and 8.1 versions prior to 8.1.4.1. Those affected organizations running Rails applications that use ImageMagick for Active Storage image processing run with a security policy that does not block the affected coders, and accept uploads from untrusted users.

Active Storage patches are available now. The GitHub page includes further mitigation requirements for applications that use MiniMagick and ImageMagick.

Additionally, if an application stores attachments through the Disk service, users should "change secret_key_base and every secret readable by the application process, including the master key and everything config/credentials.yml.enc holds, storage and database credentials, and third-party tokens," the GitHub advisory said. Users should not retain exposed secrets as a fallback.

If a user requires a workaround, the GitHub page offers system rules the user can apply into ImageMagick's system policy.

Related:Claude Model Builds Exploits Across Major Browsers & OSes

Jake Knott, head of threat intelligence for exposure management vendor watchTowr, tells Dark Reading the firm has already reproduced the vulnerability in a lab environment, and "that against the right target, exploitation won't be difficult at all."

Moreover, he says he'd be surprised if public proof-of-concept exploit code took more than a few days to come to light.

"For attackers, reproducing the vulnerability here is likely the easy part, as the patterns and primitives involved in exploitation are well established," Knott says. "The hard part is finding software and deployments that are common enough, and configured in a vulnerable way, to deliver impact at scale indiscriminately."

Alex is an award-winning writer, journalist, and podcast host based in Boston. After cutting his teeth writing for independent gaming publications as a teenager, he graduated from Emerson College in 2016 with a Bachelor of Science in journalism. He has previously been published on VentureFizz, Search Security, Nintendo World Report, and elsewhere.

One of the basic problems of spending too much time on social media is that you are cooking your brain by reading posts from a pack of attention-seeking lunatics. If your social media of choice is the site formerly known as Twitter, and it is your main source of contact with the outside world, you may delusionally throw away nearly $500 million dollars on midterm elections, just like Elon Musk.

In particular, $47 million of that money from Musk’s America PAC was aimed at defeating Texas Democrat James Talarico, who trounced Musk’s preferred Senate candidate, Ken Paxton, with 55 percent of the vote. Talarico even made Musk’s influence a central part of his campaign ads, with the slogan “Texas is not for sale.”

The opposition to data centers is a way of making concrete how pissed off most people are with the tech industry

How did Musk get this so wrong? I blame the bubble of X. My former colleague Casey Newton, who has (with some well-earned shame) slunk back to X, explained that he did so because it was the site that was most influential with the AI crowd. In some sense, this is exactly the problem. The agentmaxxing loons of AI influencing are wildly out of touch with normal people. There is, right now, one very important bipartisan position in the United States, and it is: Fuck a data center.

It’s pretty obvious that the opposition to data centers is a way of making concrete how pissed off most people are with the tech industry, particularly with the gangster tech regulation of late and the constant stream of threats coming from AI companies. These guys have really tied themselves and their AI aspirations to Donald Trump’s sinking ship, and if you’re hanging out on X all the time, that’s impossible to see, not least because the site’s owner, Musk, is shaping the algorithm in ways that make him feel good.

Since Musk seems to have forgotten what it is like to experience joy, or even mild satisfaction, I have taken the liberty to make a list of things he could have bought to get him a better return on his investment. He probably could flip several things on this list for more money, but at minimum he could have gotten a little rush of pleasure from a new purchase:

But hey, at least changing the name of SpaceX’s “Superintelligence” division back to “Artificial Intelligence” is free!

Musk spent last night deleting tweets so he wouldn’t look as stupid as he is. Too bad it’s not as easy to delete other things! Texans were already asking questions about sweetheart deals to benefit Musk, and now there’s a wave of politicians who ran against his money coming into power, including the governor and the attorney general of Texas. That may mean unredacting Gov. Greg Abbott’s Musk emails, for instance — answering some questions about the influence Musk bought with Texas Republicans.

So actually, maybe the best investment on that list would be the dream team of lawyers who clobbered Musk on the Twitter deal — because Musk has a bunch of new enemies in power, and the simplest thing in the world they can do to curry favor with their voters is investigate him. Bet you’d never know that if you spent all your time on X.

Toptal: ML Inference Engineer – Terminal-Bench Task Designer

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

We are building a benchmark that tests how well AI agents handle real expert work in a terminal. We are hiring ML inference and serving experts to turn real, paid tasks from their jobs into expert-level Terminal-Bench challenges. Each task includes the environment, a working solution, and an automated check that proves the result is correct.

***THIS IS A TASK BASED COMPENSATION MODEL. PLEASE CHECK THE APPLICATION QUESTIONS FOR MORE DETAILS.***

• Design expert-level Terminal-Bench tasks that turn real, paid ML inference work from your own experience into challenges an AI agent must solve end to end.

• Define the files, systems and environments each task runs on, such as model serving with streaming, so the challenge is realistic, reproducible and command-line solvable.

• Write a working reference solution for every task and verify it runs end to end, sized for about 60 to 75 minutes of expert effort.

• Build automated checks that objectively prove a result is correct, including cases where ML outputs go wrong silently, such as numerical drift or metric bugs.

• Package tasks with Docker and Git so they are reproducible, and document the systems, tools, data and rules involved so reviewers can validate difficulty.

• Iterate on tasks based on reviewer feedback and agent test runs, adjusting difficulty and fixing ambiguities so each task stays expert-level, fair and objectively gradable.

• 3+ years doing ML inference or serving work hands-on (not only studying, selling or managing it), or a PhD plus active research in the subfield

• Did this work in the last 3 years and can name the systems, tools, data and rules used

• Trained, served or evaluated models at real scale (multi-GPU or production serving), not only notebooks or API calls

• Hands-on with PyTorch or JAX internals and serving stacks such as vLLM, SGLang or TensorRT-LLM

• Can explain how ML results go wrong without errors (numerical drift, metric bugs) and how to catch them

• Technical fluency in Python and the command line, and with files, CSV/JSON, APIs or databases

• Availability to start by early November 2026 and complete tasks of about 60 to 75 minutes each

• AI lab or LLM infrastructure experience

• Contributions to PyTorch, vLLM or SGLang

• Task vetting instead of an interview: complete a vetting task so the client can assess your task design

• Paid per task; each task takes about 60 to 75 minutes

• Work is delivered task by task, with reviewer feedback on submissions

No forms. Just smart, optimized applications.

That Hole in Your Ruler Isn’t Random – Here’s What It’s Actually For

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

That small hole on a ruler has a practical purpose and an unexpected history. Here's why it exists and how people use it.

Almost everyone has used a ruler at some point, whether it was for a school project, measuring something around the house, or drawing a straight line. But even after years of using one, there’s a small detail that’s easy to miss: the hole near the end.

Those holes in rulers aren’t just part of the design. They actually serve a purpose, and the reason they’re there goes back further than most people might expect. Here’s what they’re for, how they were used in the past, and a few other ways they come in handy.

The hole at the end of a ruler is mainly there for storage. It lets people hang the ruler on a hook, nail, or pegboard instead of leaving it in a drawer or on a workbench. This is particularly helpful with longer metal rulers that might not fit in a toolbox.

But the hole has been around for much longer than modern workshops. Years ago, before paper notebooks became common in schools, students practiced writing on small slate boards. They could wipe away their writing with a damp cloth and use the board again.

Some students tied that cloth to their rulers with a piece of string through the hole. That way, they always had something nearby to clean their slates. Schools eventually moved away from slate boards, but the holes remained. Today, they’re mostly there to make rulers easier to hang and store.

Hanging a ruler isn’t the only reason manufacturers add holes. Some rulers have several, and they can serve different purposes depending on where they’re placed. For example, certain school rulers have three holes spaced along one side. These line up with the rings in a standard three-ring binder, so students can keep their rulers with their other school supplies.

Some rulers also have holes that help with drawing circles. A pin through one hole can hold the ruler in place while a pencil in another hole traces a circle as the ruler turns. Not every ruler has holes designed for this, but specialty models like the Creative Grids Quick Trim Ruler do. A compass is still a better choice when exact measurements matter.

Some crafting rulers have holes for more specific jobs. Quilting rulers and templates may have openings that help mark circles or line up fabric before cutting. Others have slots or shaped edges that guide a rotary cutter, making it easier to cut certain shapes.

There are also special rulers for paper crafts and stitching. Some have rows of tiny, evenly spaced holes that help people mark where to make stitches or decorative patterns.

Of course, not every ruler has holes. Smaller plastic rulers often don’t need them, while longer metal versions may have one at each end. It mostly comes down to how the manufacturer expects people to use the ruler. And for everyday measuring, the holes don’t make much difference. A ruler without them can measure just as accurately as one with them.

We are no longer supporting IE (Internet Explorer) as we strive to provide site experiences for browsers that support new web standards and security practices.

We recommend our users to update the browser.

VMware releases vSphere Express for virtualization on up to 128 cores

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

“We started listening to a lot of our lower-end customers,” VMware exec says.

After alienating many long-time small- to medium-sized business (SMB) customers, Broadcom’s VMware is trying to appeal to its smaller customers.

Today, the virtualization company announced vSphere Express, a lower-end virtualization offering for organizations that require “cost-effective essential virtualization.”

vSphere Express comes after many customers reported that VMware was pushing them to VMware Cloud Foundation (VCF), the flagship private cloud bundle that has many features SMBs don’t need and is expensive. Some SMB users have reported that VMware sales representatives previously told them that vSphere Standard, VMware’s prior entry-level virtualization offering, wasn’t available anymore.

A VMware spokesperson told The Register that VMware will sell a per-core subscription license at a price that will be “significantly lower” than the VMware vSphere Foundation (VVF) bundle and VCF but wouldn’t provide a price quote.

IT distributor TD Synnex lists vSphere Express as costing $9,600 a year for running on up to 128 cores. The price doesn’t get cheaper with fewer cores, which still presents a challenge to the smallest use cases.

vSphere Express includes basic capabilities, like vMotion, for “live migration of running virtual machines with improved migration speed and efficiency,” and high availability for the “automated restart of failed virtual machines,” VMware’s announcement said.

The product only supports discrete instances, and organizations won’t be able to manage them centrally, Prashanth Shenoy, VP of product marketing for VCF, said, according to The Register.

“We started listening to a lot of our lower-end customers who didn’t really want the VVF but wanted the core virtualization capability, and [vSphere Express is] really meant for that market,” Shenoy said, per SDxCentral.

Whereas vSphere Standard hasn’t seen a meaningful update in years, vSphere Express will be updated as frequently as VCF and VVF are, The Register reported.

Returning focus to a streamlined low-end virtualization product is an about-face for VMware, which has largely focused on enterprise-size customers since Broadcom’s takeover. This focus shift has been evident as the tech giant has reduced the number of channel partners that it works with, bundled its product lineup into a small number of expensive SKUs, and canceled plans to take its biggest clients direct.

While VMware has been financially successful under Broadcom, vSphere Express suggests Broadcom may worry about losing business from smaller organizations and, more broadly, that its changes are pushing customers of various sizes to explore their options.

In September, while teasing what would become vSphere Express, Paul Turner, VCF’s chief product officer, told The Register that Broadcom issued sales incentives that pushed its sales team to sell VCF. He said that VMware had “too big a focus on VCF.”

Nick Watson, chief technologist at UK-based tech advisory firm C4C Group, told TechTarget today: “My reading is that Express exists because customers looked at VCF, did the sums, and started packing. This is Broadcom putting a door back on the building.”

Migrating off VMware can be very costly and time-consuming, making matters particularly complicated for SMBs. For organizations that can’t currently juggle a migration alongside other projects and/or have limited resources, vSphere Express can be a much simpler and more easily attainable solution than changing virtualization platforms.

Some SMBs, though, including the thousands of firms that have already migrated to VMware rivals and firms whose preferred resellers no longer work with VMware, will be much harder to sway, though.

“Broadcom has lost all credibility. No thanks. We have two more locations to migrate off VMware and we’re done with this nightmare. Not playing these games,” a Reddit user said last week in response to details shared about vSphere Express.

Speaking with Ars Technica in September, Dean Colpitts, CTO of Canadian managed services provider and former VMware partner Members IT Group, noted that even though migrating to the new low-end virtualization offering may be easy, Broadcom has “already shown both customers and partners they can’t be trusted.”

With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Norway Gave Us Skiing—and the Gear to Match

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Here’s something to share during après chatter: The word “ski” comes from Norwegian, and archaeologists trace the prehistoric origins of the sport to the Sami people, who used skis for hunting and travel across what would become Scandinavia. Later, the Norwegian military turned the practice into a sport, complete with races. No wonder Norway dominates so many ski disciplines at the winter Olympics.

It’s also no wonder that Helly Hansen, the legendary weather-protection gear maker, extended their apparel line to ski gear after developing waterproof oilskins almost 150 years ago. With a relentless focus on developing new materials and designs that improve comfort and performance, Helly Hansen’s latest products—like these four pieces—are the season’s secret weapon for getting the most fun out of the snow.

Inventing skiing and the best winter gear should be enough. But to really seal its legacy as the champion of the sport, Helly Hansen also wants you to Ski Free. With the purchase of an eligible pair of pants or jacket, get a complimentary one-day ski pass at one of more than 80 participating ski resorts worldwide.

One of the best jackets for resort skiing gets even better this year. The Men’s Alpha/Women’s Alphelia is lighter, warmer, and even more breathable, thanks to upgraded Lifaloft synthetic insulation that packs down tighter without sacrificing water resistance or performance. Just-right details (detachable helmet-compatible hood and powder skirt, ski pass pocket, underarm vents) now include high-tech safety features like the Life Pocket+, which helps keep your phone alive in the cold, and a RECCO Advanced Rescue System to help others find you in an emergency.

When it comes to gear, you have to earn the name “Legendary”—and these best-selling pants do, with features and flex that adapt to unpredictable conditions. Two-way stretch helps you dig into deep turns without feeling restricted, and the Helly Tech Performance shell fabric locks out precip while shedding moisture from the inside. Primaloft insulation keeps you plenty warm on long lift rides, but you can also dump heat: Lengthy thigh zippers offer venting for strenuous uphill climbs or leg-burning descents on warmer days.

Everyone needs a versatile midlayer. Make yours this one—it transitions perfectly from shoulder-season hiking to winter skiing. In place of conventional pile-and-pill fleece, the unique grid-pattern fleece traps heat and breathes well, keeping you in the comfort zone through changes in temperature and effort without having to drop or swap a layer. A handy chest pocket and slim, stylish profile round out the all-purpose design.

Ever heard a ski patroller complain about cold fingers? Of course not—they know they need gloves that work for both warmth and dexterity, which is why Helly Hansen teamed up with top ski patrol teams to design a glove that does the job all day, even in horrendous conditions. Fully waterproof, breathable, and insulated with Primaloft Black Eco, the Odin glove has reinforced overlays in high-wear areas like the palm and forefingers to increase grip and durability. Fleece lining ensures an oh-so-soft fit, and included carabiner and finger loops keep these gloves out of the snow and on your belt when off.

Founded in Norway in 1877, Helly Hansen has been making professional grade gear to help people stay and feel alive in the harshest environments around the world. Helly Hansen is a leader in performance ski apparel and technical sailing, as well as premium outdoor gear.

US stocks post best day since August after Democrats sweep midterms

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Hyperscalers surge and chipmakers fall as investors bet on winners and losers from expected backlash against the AI boom

→ Baca artikel asli

‘Seismic’ blue wave triggers the sinking of oil prices, energy stocks, and the chances of power permitting reform

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Most energy stocks plunged on Wednesday—except for clean power and utilities in a few remaining reliably red states—as crude oil prices fell to one-month lows because of bullishness on Iran war peace talks and bearishness on the data center boom after Democratic blue wave wins in the midterm elections.

Even as Wall Street celebrated the anticipated gridlock from a splintered Congress and White House—the Dow Jones Industrial Average rose 3% Wednesday—most energy companies moved in the opposite direction, especially in states such as Texas that had the most shocking Democratic wins.

The other potential loser is the bipartisan legislation to expedite the construction of energy projects—and power the AI boom—that would benefit both renewable power and oil and gas pipelines. Anti-fossil fuel Democrats are now potentially motivated to take harder negotiating tactics. TD Cowen analysts slashed their projection for passing the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act (BAAJA) from 40% to 25% during the lame-duck session before the new Congress is sworn in.

Charles Hua, executive director of the PowerLines nonprofit, said the “seismic” blue wave from Congress to governors’ elections to public service commissions was a referendum on spiking utility bills, gasoline costs, and the inflationary ripple effects throughout the economy.

“You only get such seismicity in political results if something is fundamentally unsteady or unstable, and I think one of the big manifestations of that really has been energy costs,” Hua told Fortune.

In addition to voter discontent over the Iran war and fuel prices, there were more than 40 local ballot measure on restricting or regulating data centers. All but one of them—in North Dakota—passed. “That’s a staggering statistic,” Hua said.

As for Wall Street performance, there were clear winners and losers by utilities and independent power producers.

NRG Energy and Vistra, which are building out gas-fired power generation in Texas to help fuel the AI boom, saw their stock values drop precipitously by 12% and 15%, respectively. Gas turbine manufacturer GE Vernova dipped by 10%.

Analyst Julien Dumoulin-Smith of Jefferies specifically highlighted the “Texas shock,” noting that “the most impactful surprise is the magnitude by which Texas voted Democratic, imperiling the outlook for data center developments.”

Texas-based Tesla and SpaceX leader Elon Musk—after unsuccessfully spending hundreds of millions on GOP candidates—posted on X, “Data centers in space looking pretty appealing right now.”

On the other hand, utilities that operate in Florida, which remained Republican, were rewarded. Florida-based NextEra Energy jumped 5% and Duke Energy rose by 3%.

With more clean energy-loving Democrats coming into power, renewable energy companies also were rewarded. First Solar rose by over 4% and solar parts manufacturer Nextpower spiked by 13%.

After President Donald Trump downplayed the potential for an escalation in Iran and Vice President JD Vance blamed his party’s election losses on the war, the global oil benchmark fell by 8% to below $100 per barrel for the first time in nearly a month. As such, oil producers and refiners suffered on Wall Street too.

ExxonMobil and Chevron dipped by 4% and 5%, respectively. Refiners Marathon Petroleum and Valero Energy, which have reported record profits of late, plunged by 9% and 7%, respectively.

“We had a referendum on inflation, high oil prices, and Trump in general,” said oil forecaster Dan Pickering, founder of the Pickering Energy Partners consulting and research firm. “It feels like Trump will have more pushback and less maneuverability around how he wants to conduct the conflict in Iran.”

There may be optimism for peace talks in Iran, but this war likely is far from over, Pickering warned.

There is no clear compromise yet, and Trump could easily get frustrated and opt for escalation between now and January, which is what Trump himself predicted before the election.

“I think the Iran conflict gets more complicated for the U.S. We’ve been looking for an off ramp, but we want an off ramp that doesn’t leave the Middle East in chaos,” Pickering told Fortune. “I think that issue probably transcends the election. Even with more Democrats, I don’t think they’re going to want a bad outcome for the Iran crisis. Oil is down, but it’s not cratering.”

Domestically, much of the focus will remain on the data center backlash and permitting reform. Some of the biggest states for data center growth are Texas, Georgia, and Ohio and Democratic challengers all won the governor elections in those states while pushing for data center moratoriums.

“‘Moratorium’ certainly is the buzzword,” Hua said, but it’s not clear if those efforts equate to temporary pauses or outright opposition. “How high on the priory list is electricity? I suspect that electricity vis-a-vis data centers will remain a top-of-mind issue for these new administrations.”

The AI boom has brought both parties somewhat closer together in terms of supporting all-of-the-above energy solutions. That’s why a bipartisan permitting reform effort focused on expediting energy construction of all kinds was coming together. But a Democratic wave is expected to leave more of them arguing in favor of waiting until next year to propose a version that’s less friendly to fossil fuel projects. Such an effort could be risky because it would still require Trump’s signature.

“With greater leverage heading into 2027, Democrats have less incentive to accept pipeline provisions during the lame duck, pushing the debate toward a narrower clean energy-transmission package or no deal,” Dumoulin-Smith added.

The legislation would facilitate the growth of long-distance transmission lines, which most benefit renewable energy. As such, top transmission developer Quanta Services saw its stock fall by 12% Wednesday.

The legislation’s Democratic sponsor, Sen. Sheldon Whitehouse, D-R.I., implored his party to still support the bill on Wednesday.

“Democrats ran and won on affordability, and this bill lowers costs for families and will allow us to start delivering on our election promises,” Whitehouse told Fortune in a statement. “It’s a bird in the hand. We’d be fools not to take it.”

Jordan Blum is the Energy editor at Fortune, overseeing coverage of a growing global energy sector for oil and gas, transition businesses, renewables, and critical minerals.

Five Hacks Every Una Watch User Should Know

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Una watch is pretty much made to be hackable, with its interchangeable parts and open development environment. But it’s also pretty barebones at launch, without many features available yet. However, here are five things I’ve discovered I can do with it—and I hope to expand this list quite a bit as the device and its community mature.

Because the Una watch has a regular USB-C port, you don’t need to remember any specific cable, dongle, or adapter to charge it. I knew that, but it didn’t occur to me until recently that I didn’t need a computer or a wall outlet to plug into the other end. I was able to take a USB-C to USB-C cable—the same kind I might carry around to charge my phone—and plug one end into my watch and the other into my phone. It charged!

(You can also charge some other devices from a phone, including an Apple Watch, but you’d need to carry around that specific adapter, which is why nobody bothers.)

The Una watch doesn’t have any built-in sleep tracking, but a few community-built apps aim to provide it. The Slumber app, for example, will let you know how long you slept and compare your most recent bedtime with when you usually get to bed. There are no sleep stages (honestly: refreshing, since watches are rarely good at this anyway), and the analysis is done on the watch itself.

But if what you’re really hoping for is a recovery score or an overnight HRV reading, the HabitLab app combines that with habit tracking. Every evening, the watch will buzz and ask you to check off which habits or factors played into your day: Alcohol and late meals are among the options, and the watch will automatically detect morning or evening workouts to add them to the list. In the morning, the watch asks you how you slept, and then it gives you a score based on your resting heart rate, HRV, and your subjective rating.

The app is supposed to be able to put together trends and insights from this data, but I haven’t managed to get it to do this. Maybe it’s waiting for more data (I’ve been using it a little over a week), or maybe it’s just buggy.

By contrast, the barcode app worked even better than I expected it to. It’s meant for a parkrun barcode; each participant in the free weekly 5K has a number that you can use to check your results online. But it works just as well for other barcodes.

I installed the Barcode app and entered the number for my library card. Perhaps because of the watch’s MIP screen, I found this barcode scanned more reliably than any barcodes I’ve previously tried to use on a watch. The library scanner picked it up just fine on the first try, and a phone-based scanner also had no problem with it. You can store up to six barcodes, so I’ve also entered a few others, like my grocery discount card.

The app supports QR codes as well as regular barcodes, and to enter a QR code, you just type in a shortened URL. (It needs to be 22 characters or less.) This means that the Barcode app is also a QR code creator, a nifty feature indeed.

Seeing how soon the sun sets is a bit of a downer as we leave daylight saving time and get into the dark winter months, but it’s also helpful in planning. The Sun & Moon Face puts a graphic on your wrist that shows how far along the sun is in its trajectory across the sky for the day. When evening is approaching, you’ll see just how little time you have left.

The app grabs your GPS location once a day, which means that for the first few hours I wore it, the sunrise and sunset times were incorrectly listed as 6 a.m. and 6 p.m. But as long as you aren’t flying to a drastically new location every day, the app does the job just fine. As a bonus, it changes the color of the screen’s sky to show daylight, nighttime, or sunrise/sunset colors.

If you want to hack this hackable watch, you might as well take the plunge and try to build something yourself. Instructions are here to download the development tools for Windows, Mac, or Linux, and there are tutorials for different features you might want to use and implement. There are also about a dozen example apps you can build on, including activity trackers, watch faces, and glances. I'm not much of a coder myself, but this is the friendliest setup I've seen for someone to get into building their own apps for a watch.

The Art of the Panoramic Aspect Ratio

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Panoramic compositions offer another way to present a scene, preserving its horizontal breadth and giving the frame a different sense of scale, movement, and visual balance. They can be extremely powerful.

The proportions of a photograph influence how it is viewed. A conventional landscape frame provides a relatively balanced relationship between horizontal and vertical space, while a panoramic crop places considerably more emphasis on width. This changes the viewer’s experience, encouraging the eye to move across the image and take in the composition progressively.

That extended viewing experience is one of the format’s greatest strengths. Rather than concentrating attention within a relatively compact frame, a panoramic photograph gives visual information more room to unfold. The viewer can follow changes in light, examine relationships between foreground and background elements, and appreciate the full extent of the scene. This can make the photograph feel expansive without relying on dramatic processing or exaggerated perspective.

Panoramic images also have a distinctive sense of balance. Their proportions can create a feeling of openness and continuity, especially when the composition has a strong horizontal structure. The format can be quiet and understated or dramatic and imposing, depending on the subject, light, and arrangement of elements. It is not simply a way to include more of a scene. It changes which parts of the photograph receive emphasis and how the viewer moves through it.

The panoramic format works best when a scene’s horizontal breadth is an important part of its visual appeal. Landscape photography is an obvious application, but the format can also work well for architecture, cityscapes, environmental portraits, and other subjects where the surrounding space contributes to the image.

Before choosing a panoramic composition, consider what makes the original photograph compelling. If the subject depends on vertical elements or a strong relationship between foreground and sky, removing too much of the image’s height may weaken it. If the strongest visual information is distributed horizontally, however, a wider frame may create a more cohesive composition.

The format can also help simplify a photograph. Cropping away unnecessary sky or foreground reduces competing information and emphasizes the remaining elements. This is particularly useful when the original image contains substantial areas that contribute little to its visual impact. However, removing space simply to achieve a wider aspect ratio is not necessarily an improvement. Open areas can provide balance, establish scale, and give important details room within the frame.

It is worth comparing several aspect ratios before deciding on a final composition. A moderately wide image may preserve enough vertical information to maintain depth, while a more elongated format can create a stronger emphasis on horizontal movement. Neither is inherently better. The right proportions support the composition without sacrificing the information that matters.

A panoramic composition requires attention across the entire width of the frame. In a conventional photograph, the viewer may be drawn quickly toward a central subject. With a panoramic image, the extended proportions create more opportunity for visual movement, but they also make balance particularly important. A strong focal point should work with the rest of the frame rather than simply occupy the center.

Horizontal lines and directional elements can help establish that movement. The placement of the horizon, the alignment of natural or architectural features, and the distribution of contrasting areas all influence how the viewer moves through the photograph. Consider whether the composition has a clear visual starting point and whether the remaining elements support or interrupt that progression.

Negative space plays an equally important role. A wide frame does not need to be filled with detail from edge to edge. Open areas can provide visual relief, separate competing elements, and establish the atmosphere of the photograph. Removing too much space during cropping can make an image feel crowded, particularly when important subjects sit close to the boundaries.

Pay as much attention to the left and right edges as to the center. Small distractions become more noticeable when the composition is stretched horizontally, and an element that seems insignificant in the original frame may disrupt the finished image. Review the entire composition before releasing the shutter, and make small adjustments to camera position when necessary. The goal is not to fill every part of the frame, but to make each area feel intentional.

A wide aspect ratio can communicate a scene’s breadth, but width alone does not create depth. The relationship between elements at different distances remains essential. Overlapping forms, changes in contrast, and variations in light help establish separation and give the viewer a sense of how the scene extends into the distance.

Atmospheric conditions can be particularly effective in this regard. Haze, fog, rain, and changing light create tonal differences between near and distant elements. Preserving those transitions across a panoramic composition can give the photograph a strong sense of depth without requiring an exaggerated foreground.

Scale also benefits from thoughtful composition. Including recognizable elements within an expansive environment provides a reference point that helps viewers understand the size of the surrounding space. The challenge is to retain enough context for that relationship to be clear without allowing secondary details to compete with the main subject.

When editing, consider how much of the original foreground and background needs to remain. A panoramic crop that removes too much of either can flatten the photograph or change its sense of scale. The goal is to retain the relationships that give the image depth while letting the wider proportions emphasize the overall scene.

You can create panoramic photographs with almost any focal length. The choice depends on how much of the scene you need to include and how you want the different elements to relate to one another.

Wide-angle lenses capture a broad field of view and can preserve a strong relationship between foreground and background. They are useful when the composition depends on including substantial environmental context, although perspective becomes increasingly pronounced as the camera moves closer to foreground subjects. This can make nearby elements appear much larger in relation to the background.

Standard and telephoto lenses offer a different approach. Longer focal lengths isolate a narrower portion of the scene and can make distant elements appear more prominent within the composition. They are useful when the visual interest is concentrated in a particular section of the landscape or when the photographer wants to emphasize layers without including much foreground.

Camera position is just as important as focal length. Changes in height and direction can alter the alignment of elements, the amount of foreground visible, and the relationship between different parts of the scene. Take time to assess the composition before settling on a viewpoint, especially when working with a longer lens.

If the intention is to create a panoramic crop from a single exposure, leave sufficient room around important details. Cropping reduces the available resolution and may remove more of the original frame than expected. A little extra space gives you flexibility in editing and helps prevent important elements from being cut off.

There are two primary ways to create panoramic photographs: cropping a single exposure or stitching multiple frames together. Each approach has advantages, and the best choice depends on the scene, the available resolution, and whether anything within the frame is moving.

Cropping a single exposure is often the simplest option. It works particularly well when the camera’s native field of view already includes the necessary horizontal information and the resulting file retains enough resolution for the intended output. High-resolution cameras offer more flexibility, but it is still important to consider how much of the original image you’ll remove.

Stitching multiple photographs together can produce a much larger file with additional detail. This is useful when photographing a scene that extends beyond the camera’s field of view or when you intend to print a particularly large image. For consistent results, use a tripod when practical, keep the camera level, and overlap consecutive frames by approximately 25 to 30 percent. The overlap gives stitching software enough shared detail to align the images.

Manual exposure and a fixed white balance help maintain consistency throughout the sequence. If the camera changes exposure or white balance between frames, visible variations can appear in the finished image. Focus should also remain consistent. Consider focusing once and switching to manual focus before capturing the remaining frames, particularly when photographing distant subjects.

Stitching is not always the best choice. Movement within the scene can create alignment problems, duplicated details, or visible inconsistencies. Waves, foliage, clouds, and moving subjects may change position between exposures. In these situations, a single image may produce a more natural result. When stitching, also be mindful of nearby objects, since parallax can cause alignment errors if the camera rotates around the wrong point.

Post-processing shapes the final proportions and balance of a panoramic image. Most editing programs allow photographers to select a custom crop ratio or enter specific dimensions. Ratios such as 2:1 or 2.35:1 provide useful starting points, but there is no need to force every photograph into the same proportions.

Begin by identifying the visual information that needs to remain. Consider whether the sky, foreground, and surrounding details contribute to the photograph or simply occupy space. Adjust the crop gradually and compare different proportions. A relatively small change in height can have a significant effect on the image’s balance.

Check the position of the horizon and other prominent horizontal lines. Their placement should reflect the visual importance of the areas above and below them. Also review the edges for distractions, and make sure important details aren’t positioned awkwardly close to the boundaries.

For stitched panoramas, cropping also removes the uneven edges created during alignment. Leave enough room during capture to account for this, particularly when important details extend close to the edges of the frame. Once you’ve established the composition, inspect the full-resolution image for stitching errors, uneven transitions, and inconsistencies in exposure or color.

Avoid making the crop so tight that the image loses its sense of openness. One of the advantages of the panoramic format is the space it provides. Removing too much of that space can undermine the very quality that makes the format appealing.

Large prints are one of the most compelling applications of panoramic photography. The extended proportions make effective use of horizontal wall space, creating a presentation that feels distinct from a conventional landscape print. They are particularly well suited to areas where a standard rectangular photograph might appear too small or visually contained.

The viewing experience also changes with print size. A panoramic image encourages the viewer to move their attention across its length, taking in details and relationships that might be overlooked when viewing a smaller image on a screen. At a larger scale, subtle changes in tone, texture, and light become more apparent. The photograph can become something viewers return to rather than an image they take in at a glance.

The format is also versatile in interior spaces. Its proportions can work above furniture, along hallways, in offices, or across larger uninterrupted walls. A carefully composed panoramic print can provide a strong focal point without requiring an elaborate arrangement of multiple images. Its visual impact comes from the photograph itself and the space it occupies.

Preparing an image for a large print requires more consideration than simply selecting a wide crop. Resolution is particularly important. Cropping a single exposure reduces the available pixel dimensions, so check the final file against the print lab’s recommendations before ordering. If the intended print is especially large, stitching multiple frames may provide the additional resolution needed to retain fine detail.

The viewing distance should also inform the final output. A print intended to be seen from across a room does not necessarily require the same pixel density as one that will be examined from close range. Consult the printer’s specifications and consider the physical dimensions of the finished image before making final sharpening and output decisions.

The print surface influences how the photograph is presented. Matte papers reduce reflections and suit images with subtle tonal transitions, while luster and semi-gloss finishes add more surface sheen and can emphasize contrast. Canvas offers a different aesthetic, with visible texture and a softer rendering of fine detail. Choose a finish that complements the photograph rather than treating it as an afterthought.

Framing and presentation matter, too. Panoramic aspect ratios don’t always match standard frame sizes, so custom framing may be necessary. A mat can separate the photograph from its surroundings, while a frameless presentation gives the image a more continuous appearance. Consider the available wall space and the intended viewing distance when deciding on the final dimensions.

The strongest panoramic photographs aren’t necessarily the widest or most dramatic. Their success comes from the relationship between the subject, the composition, and the proportions of the finished image. The format should give the photograph a reason to extend horizontally, whether that means preserving the breadth of a landscape, emphasizing visual relationships, or creating a more immersive viewing experience.

When composing, look beyond the central subject and consider the entire width of the scene. Pay attention to balance, negative space, depth, and the placement of important details. Leave room to refine the crop, and consider the intended presentation before removing a substantial portion of the original image.

A panoramic photograph offers more than an alternative aspect ratio. It lets you control how a scene is experienced, giving the viewer space to explore the image and allowing the photograph to take on a distinctive presence when displayed at a larger scale.

How Aussie Bitcoiners can avoid 2027’s shock CGT hike: Part 1

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

From setting up an SMSF or a company, to just leaving the country entirely, here are some strategies Australian Bitcoiners can employ to deal with 2027’s massive CGT hike.

The Australian government’s decision to scrap the 50% capital gains discount for all investments in the May budget came as a big shock for crypto owners. The new scheme effectively doubles the taxable component of a gain, minus a small discount for inflation.

But there are legal ways to minimize the amount of CGT you’ll have to pay — if you start prepping now.

Some Aussie crypto holders are taking drastic measures — like Finder founder and crypto king Fred Schebesta who is relocating to Singapore which offers 0% CGT, stating the tax changes were a “killer blow.”But there are less dramatic methods to reduce your CGT bills including setting up a self managed super fund (SMSF), a company to trade crypto at the company tax rate, or to just cross your fingers and hope the opposition will reverse the changes after the next election.

Since 1999, Aussie asset owners have only paid tax on half of their capital gain. From July 1, 2027 they’ll pay their full income tax rate — which means 30% CGT if your income and gains are between A$45,000 and $135,000, 37% for the portion between $135,000 and $190,000 and 47% above that. (All figures in this story are in Aussie dollars)

Adding insult to injury, low-income earners who aren’t on welfare like students and retirees will pay a minimum 30% CGT rate no matter how little the gain.

The only discount now available accounts for the effect of inflation on your cost basis. So if you buy a house for $1 million in 2027 and sell it for $1.5 million after 10 years, with inflation at 3%, you won’t have to pay tax on the $344,000 or so attributed to inflation.

“The challenge for cryptocurrency is that the CGT changes, indexation really helps slower growing assets not fast ones,” explains Andrea Yuen, Swyftx CEO.

So if you bought Bitcoin for $10,000 and sell it ten years later for $100,000, the inflation discount is only around $3,439. Under the old system you would pay tax on $45,000 of gains. Under the new system you’ll pay tax on $86,561 of gains.

Stockspot analyst Chris Bryki compared how ten different asset classes would fare under the new rules. “Crypto sits at the bottom because almost its entire return comes from capital growth,” he wrote in The Australian newspaper.

Here are a few different strategies to help crypto holders deal with the changes, ranging from little to no effort, to restructuring your entire life.

Any gain you’ve made before July 1 2027 will be taxed under the existing 50% discount scheme. So your best bet is to hope a raging bull market pushes the value of your portfolio as high as possible before that date.You should faithfully record the value of every crypto asset you have that day, as any gain from that value will be taxed at the higher rate.

Ben Simpson, founder of crypto advisory service Collective Shift, says Bitcoin’s performance since mid-year in a challenging macro environment bodes well for further gains before July.

But Simpson (who points out he’s not a financial advisor) adds, “I probably wouldn’t be betting the farm on the fact that we’re gonna have a huge bull run”

Yuen notes that prices aren’t within your control.  “A strategy isn’t a strategy if you’re just sort of hoping,” she says.

Both the official opposition The Coalition, and the insurgent opposition One Nation, have promised to reverse the changes. Crypto voters who feel strongly enough about this single issue, could vote for a change in government.However, the ruling ALP holds 94 out of 150 seats in the House of Representatives and will be difficult to dislodge from office. If the government does change — and the legislation can pass the Senate in time — it’s technically possible the changes could be reversed before anyone has to file taxes from July 1 2028.

But it’s probably more likely that any change to the system would occur after that date. Adrian Forza, director of Crypto Tax Australia, says this would make calculating gains very difficult.

Australian retirement funds are called superannuation funds, and once you are over 60 you can access your investments tax-free — even as they keep growing.Unfortunately none of the retail super funds allow clients to invest in crypto, so if you want to protect your crypto gains using this method, you’d have to set up an SMSF. About a third of Simpson’s high net worth clients invest in crypto via SMSFs.  “Buying Bitcoin and accumulating it there is a super solid idea,” he says, although he cautions against adding many altcoins. “You’re risking your retirement, which is an ultra long-term hold, on short-term altcoins,” he says.

Australian super fund AMP has invested in Bitcoin — but doesn’t allow its clients to do so.

The big drawback with SMSFs — explains the 27-year-old founder — is that younger crypto investors can’t access the money for decades.

Realistically then, it’s a strategy more suited to wealthier investors aged over 50, who have a shorter period to wait.

Related: Older investors are risking everything for a crypto-funded retirement

The government has also made running an SMSF a pain in the backside, with lots of compliance paperwork and audits, as well as a bunch of unavoidable fees that make it prohibitive for smaller balances. Annual costs typically range between $2,000 and $4,000, although online providers like Stake can reduce total annual costs to around $1,330, as long as you only buy Bitcoin or Ether ETFs.

You also have to sell any crypto and pay tax on it before you can rebuy it with your SMSF, and there are annual contribution limits to factor in, although Forza says you should be able to get almost $400,000 in there with a bit of creativity.

Keep an eye out in December for a detailed report on how to set up a crypto SMSF.

A strategy some billionaires employ to avoid paying tax is by borrowing against their assets, as repaying the loan often works out cheaper than paying tax on a sale.

Bitcoiners can now access a range of lenders, including BlockEarner, Veild and Swyftx, that let you employ a similar strategy by borrowing against your BTC as collateral.

“We like to bring that strategy to the masses, not just to billionaires,” laughs Yuen.

But it can be risky if you don’t have a billionaire’s deep pockets. Borrowers face high interest rates, relatively short loan periods (typically 12 months), and risk losing their Bitcoin if the market moves against them. You can’t borrow against the full value either — Swyftx has a loan-to-value ratio around 40%.”If your collateral drops below a certain amount, there will be a margin call,” she says.  “I would say it’s a bet on a price going up. I think you have to have a view that crypto will go up — or at least not go down so that you get margin-called... Otherwise, a crypto-backed loan wouldn’t make sense for you.”

Simpson doesn’t recommend this strategy due to the risks, but says it can pay off for some people. For example if you borrow at 10% interest, and Bitcoin compounds by 33% a year (as it has done in the past) you’d end up much better off than paying CGT.

But he warns it’s a leveraged bet that is best suited to sophisticated investors.

Bitcoin is down 28% over the past year, so anyone borrowing a year ago would have faced margin calls and possible liquidation as well as interest costs.

Borrowing against Bitcoin for a house deposit has become a more popular strategy. (BlockEarner)

Forza cautions that borrowing crypto can also be a CGT event in some circumstances, such as borrowing in DeFi. If you deposit ETH to Aave, it gets automatically swapped to WETH which  is a taxable event, and when you withdraw the ETH it’s another taxable event.

Borrowing from centralized lenders like BlockEarner or Swyftx is currently a grey area, and the ATO has not clarified if it’s a taxable event.”If you’re talking about [borrowing] ten grand fine. But if you’ve got three to five Bitcoin, I wouldn’t risk it because if it goes against you then you’re going to be paying tax.””Vield is the only one in Australia that’s got a private ruling that says their product is not a taxable event. No one else has got one.”Swftxy confirmed it has not got a private ruling and said clients should seek tax advice, while BlockEarner did not respond to a request for comment.

In part two of our Aussie CGT special, coming later this week, we’ll bring you the final four strategies to minimize your crypto tax bills, including setting up a company or leaving the country entirely.
